&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Next to him, or rather before him in some respects, is an author whom I will not name; yet truly all chemical <note>original: "Chymical"</note> writers are to be preferred by any person based on how much benefit they get from them. <meta>This page serves as an "Advertisement" or introductory notice to the reader. ### AN
# Advertisement. <note>original: *Advertisement*; in this period, the word meant a formal notice, notification, or explanation rather than a commercial promotion.</note>

<margin>a. In his most excellent and laborious collection of our ancient English Hermetic <note>Hermetic: Relating to alchemy and the mystical philosophy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> authors, called *Theatrum Chymicum Britannicum* (The British Chemical Theater), printed in London, 1652.</margin>

The *Compound of Alchemy* <note>A famous poem on alchemy written by George Ripley in 1471.</note>, which seems to be the source most frequently used for quotations in this book, mostly agrees with the edition published by Ralph Rabbards and printed in London in 1591 in quarto <note>Quarto: A book size created by folding a standard sheet of paper twice, forming four leaves.</note>. In that edition, the English is not as archaic as the copy published by Elias Ashmole, Esquire.<sup>a</sup> Yet I humbly believe that this commentator <note>original: *Expositor*</note> has [provided] <note>The text here is likely obscured or missing in the original due to the torn margin mentioned in the warning.</note> in quarto with his learned and ingenious observations upon the same. 

And I heartily wish that the learned philosophers of our age could prevail upon him to publish his second volume of that collection, which he had almost finished nearly twenty years ago (as I heard from his own mouth). It has lain dormant <note>original: *asleep*</note> ever since and is likely to remain so; for he is now unwilling to be persuaded to finish it, unless some worthy friend of his can influence him before the sleep of death seizes him and leaves those rare pieces of antiquity to be inevitably lost. This would be to the detriment of all philosophers and a great dishonor to the English nation.

<summary>This page provides a bibliographical note explaining that the book quotes from Ralph Rabbards' 1591 edition of George Ripley’s work. The author also expresses an urgent plea for Elias Ashmole to publish the long-delayed second volume of his famous alchemical collection, the *Theatrum Chymicum Britannicum*.</summary>

<keywords>George Ripley, Compound of Alchemy, Ralph Rabbards, Elias Ashmole, Theatrum Chymicum Britannicum, book history, alchemical literature, preservation of antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Advertisement" or notice to the reader, following the editor's comments on the various printed editions of Sir George Ripley's alchemical works and Elias Ashmole's collection.</meta>

I thought it appropriate to clarify the meaning <note>original: *sence*</note> of the old *English Verse* by changing a few words to make them more meaningful to our current speech, yet undoubtedly without deviating from the mind of the *Author*. I would not have dared to presume to make such changes, except that I felt I owed it to the *Author’s* reputation to let the intended meaning come through. Additionally, for this reason, anyone who desires to see the differences may easily compare all three versions together, as they are all published in *Print*. In performing this service, I hope I have done my duty faithfully and wronged nobody.

I also take the liberty of informing the *Reader* that in the *Exposition upon Sir George Ripley’s Preface*, found in the *Learned Sophies' Feast* <note>original: *Learned Sophies Feast*; "Sophies" refers to the "Sophi" or wise men/philosophers of alchemy.</note>, page 52, line 5, I found the word "greatest." Based on what follows on line 11, I humbly suggest (with all due respect) that it should be "meanest," as may more clearly appear from the same *Author* in his work *Secrets Revealed* <note>original: *Secrets Reveal'd*; a famous alchemical commentary usually attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, pages 62 and 63, and in *Sir George Ripley’s Fifth Gate*, stanza <note>original: *Stave*; a verse or stanza of a poem.</note> 40, line 6. 

However, because I found the phrase "greatest price" in two copies, I left it as it was, and by this *notice* <note>original: *Advertisement*</note> I submit it to the *Reader’s judgment*. I have also found that in the

## Sir GEORGE RIPLEY’S
# LETTER <note>original: "EPISTLE"</note>
#### TO
## King Edward the Fourth,
### EXPLAINED <note>original: "UNFOLDED"</note>.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring scrolling foliate and vine patterns.</image-desc>

This letter, as it was written directly to a King who was, in his generation, both wise and valiant, comprises the whole secret <note>This refers to the "Great Work" of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>. It is both learnedly described and yet skillfully veiled <note>original: "artificially vailed"; in this context, "artificially" means "by the rules of the Art of Alchemy"</note>. However, just as the author testifies that in this letter he plainly unties the main knot <note>meaning he clarifies the central difficulty or mystery of the process</note>, so I can and do testify with him that there is nothing more desirable for the

<summary>This introductory page presents a commentary on Sir George Ripley's alchemical letter to King Edward IV, asserting that the text contains the complete, though skillfully hidden, secrets of alchemy.</summary>

<header>An Exposition upon</header>

<margin>A vertical line is drawn in the left margin alongside the text block.</margin>

true attainment of this mystery, both in the theory and practice <note>original: "Theory and Practick"</note> of it, which is not fully taught in this short letter. This, then, I intend as a key to all my former writings; and I assure you on my faithful word that I shall not speak one word doubtfully or mystically, as I have in all my other writings—where I seemed to assert some things which, if taken literally <note>original: "without a Figure"; meaning without understanding the metaphorical or symbolic language</note>, are utterly false. We did this only to conceal this art <note>Alchemy was known as "the Art"</note>. 

Therefore, we do not intend to make this key common knowledge; and I shall entreat you to keep it secret to yourself and not to communicate it, except to a trusted friend who you are confident will not make it public. We make this request for very good reasons, knowing that all our other writings together are nothing compared to this one, because of the contradictions which we have woven into them—which is not done here in the slightest measure. 

I shall, therefore, in this letter <note>original: "Epistle"</note> adopt a new method, different from my former ones: I shall first summarize the substance of the philosophy contained in this letter into several conclusions, and afterward explain <note>original: "elucidate"</note> them.

### Sir G. Ripley’s Epistle.

<sig>A 3</sig>

The first conclusion is drawn from the ninth stanza <note>original: "Stave"</note> of this letter—the first eight stanzas being only introductory <note>original: "complementall"</note>. That conclusion is: just as all things are multiplied according to their own kind, so may metals be multiplied, as they have within themselves a capacity for being transmuted from the imperfect into the perfect.

The second conclusion in the tenth stanza <note>original: "Stave"</note> is: the main ground for the possibility of transmutation is the possibility of reducing all metals, and such minerals as are made of metallic principles, into their first mercurial matter.

The third conclusion is in the eleventh stanza <note>original: "Stave"</note>: among so many *metallic* <note>original: "Metaline"</note> and *mineral sulfurs*, and so many *mercuries*, there are but two *sulfurs* that are related to our work; these *sulfurs* have their *mercuries* essentially united to them.

The fourth conclusion from the same stanza <note>original: "Stave"</note> is: he who understands these two *sulfurs* and *mercuries* rightly shall find that the one is the most pure red *sulfur* of *gold*, which is sulfur in the manifest <note>original: "Sulphur in manifesto"; meaning the sulfurous qualities are visible or active</note> and mercury in the hidden <note>original: "Mercurius in occulto"; meaning the mercurial qualities are internal or potential</note>, and that the other is

<summary>The author outlines the first four principles of Ripley's alchemy, asserting that metals can be multiplied like biological organisms and must be reduced to their "first matter" to be transmuted.</summary>

These conclusions are few in number but are of great weight or importance <note>original: "concernment"</note>. # Sir George Ripley's Epistle

**STANZA IX.** <note>original: "STAVE IX."</note> In the 1591 Edition; but in Esquire Ashmole's *Theatrum* <note>original: *Theatrum Chemicum Britannicum*, a famous collection of English alchemical poetry published by Elias Ashmole in 1652</note> it is Stanza 8.

*But despite the danger that might occur,*
*If I do not dare to clearly untie the knot <note>meaning: reveal the central secret of the "philosopher's stone"</note> here,*
*Still, in my writing, I will not be so cryptic,*
*But that through study you may find the true knowledge*
*Of how every thing is multiplied according to its kind,*
*And how the forms of metallic bodies <note>original: "Bodies Metalline"</note> can be transformed;*
*I will declare that if you understand my meaning,*
*You shall find my writing true, and no made-up story.*

Regarding the first point: since it is not our purpose here to invite anyone to the Art <note>meaning: the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy</note>, but only intending to lead and guide the sons of Art <note>a traditional term for dedicated practitioners or "disciples" of alchemy</note>, we shall not prove the possibility of alchemy with many arguments, having already done so abundantly in another treatise. He, then, who chooses to be skeptical, let him be skeptical;

## STANZA X. <note>original: "STAVE"</note>

*As the Philosopher <note>Aristotle was frequently referred to simply as "The Philosopher" in medieval and early modern texts</note> in the* Book of Meteors <note>original: *Meteorologica*, a treatise by Aristotle</note>
*writes,*
*The form of metallic bodies is not*
*transmutable,*
*But afterward he added these words of more*
*delight,*
*Unless they are reduced to their original*
*material state,*
*Therefore such bodies which in nature are*
*meltable <note>original: "liquiable"</note>,*
*Mineral and metallic, may be turned into Mercury <note>original: "Mercurizate"</note>,*
*Understand that this science is not a matter of*
*opinion,*
*But very true, as determined by Raymond <note>Raymond Lully (c. 1232–1315), a major figure in alchemical literature</note> and others.*

We come to the second conclusion; the substance of which is that all metals, and substances with metallic principles, may be reduced to their first mercurial matter. And this is the main and chief foundation for the possibility of transmutation <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing one element or metal into another, specifically base metals into gold</note>. We must insist on this point extensively and fully, for (believe me) this is the very hinge upon which our secrets turn.

<meta>This page continues the "Epistle" of Sir George Ripley, expanding on the chemical composition of metals. It focuses on the relationship between Mercury (the "next matter" of metals) and Sulphur, explaining how these principles interact to create fixed or volatile substances.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>

-># Sir G. Ripley’s Epistle.<-

First, then, know that all metals and various minerals have Mercury as their proximate matter <term>proximate matter</term> <note>The immediate substance from which a thing is derived; in alchemy, Mercury was considered the "mother" of all metals</note>. To this Mercury (for the most part, or indeed always) there adheres and is con-coagulated <note>clotted or solidified together</note> an external *Sulphur*. This Sulphur is not metallic, but is distinguishable from the internal kernel of the *Mercury*.

This *Sulphur* is not lacking even in common *Quicksilver* <note>original: *Argent Vive*</note>. Through its mediation, the Mercury may be precipitated into the form of a dry powder. Indeed, by using a liquid well known to us (though it does not help the art of *transmutation*), it may be so fixed <term>fixed</term> <note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy means to make it non-volatile so that it stays in the crucible even under intense heat</note> that it can endure all fires, the test, and the cupel <note>original: *coppel*; a shallow porous cup used in assaying to separate precious metals from lead</note>. This is achieved without adding anything to it except the liquid (by virtue of which it is fixed), which comes away entirely intact, both in its weight <note>original: *Pondus*</note> and its power. 

This *Sulphur* is pure in gold and silver, but less pure in other metals. Therefore, in gold and silver, it is fixed; in others, it is volatile <note>original: "fugitive"</note>. In all the metals, it is solidified <note>original: "coagulated"</note>; in *Mercury* or *Quicksilver*, it is capable of being solidified. In *gold*, *silver*, and *Mercury*, this *Sulphur* is so strongly united that the *ancients* did

<keywords>Mercury, Sulphur, Argent Vive, Quicksilver, Transmutation, Fixed, Volatile, Cupel, Alchemical Principles</keywords>

...always judge *Sulphur* and *Mercury* to be one and the same. However, we use a *liquid*—the invention of which in this part of the world we owe to *Paracelsus* <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541) who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note> (though among the *Moors* and *Arabs*, it has been and still is today commonly known to the more skilled *chemists*). 

By this liquid, I say, we know that the *Sulphur* which is capable of being solidified <note>original: "coagulable"</note> in *Mercury*, and is already solidified in the *Metals*, is external to the internal nature of *Mercury*. It may be separated in the form of a colored metallic *oil* <note>original: "tincted Metallick Oyl"</note>. The remaining *Mercury* is then empty of all *Sulphur*, except for that which may be called its "Inward" or "Central *Sulphur*." On its own, this mercury cannot now be solidified (though it is to be solidified by our *Elixir*). By itself, it can neither be fixed <term>fixed</term> <note>Made resistant to evaporation by heat</note>, nor precipitated, nor sublimed <term>sublimed</term> <note>Turned into vapor and then back into a solid</note>; instead, it remains unaltered in all corrosive acids <note>original: "waters"</note> and in all applications of heat <note>original: "digestions of heat"</note>. 

One way, then, of *Mercury* transforming <term>Azating</term> <note>original: "Azating"; likely derived from "Azoth," referring to the process of reducing a metal to its essential, primordial mercurial state using a universal solvent</note> all *Metals* and *Minerals* is by the *Liquor Alchahest* <term>Alchahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body into its primary matter</note>. From all such bodies that have *Mercury* in their composition, this liquor can separate a flowing *quicksilver* <note>original: *Argent Vive*</note>. From that *quicksilver*, all its *Sulphur* is then separated, except...

...that only which is internal and central to the *Mercury*. No corrosive acid can touch this internal *Sulphur* of *Mercury*. 

In addition to this method of universal reduction <term>reduction</term> <note>In alchemy, the process of returning a metal to its original, liquid mercurial state</note>, there are also other specific ways by which lead <note>original: *Saturn*</note>, tin <note>original: *Jupiter*</note>, *antimony*, and even copper <note>original: *Venus*</note> and iron <note>original: *Mars*</note> may be reduced into a liquid *quicksilver* with the help of salts. Because these salts are physical substances <note>original: corporeal</note>, they do not penetrate as deeply <note>original: radically</note> as the previously mentioned liquid <note>the Alchahest</note> does. Therefore, they do not strip the *Mercury* of its *Sulphur*. 

Instead, just as much *Sulphur* as is found in common *Mercury* is also found in this *Mercury* of the bodies <note>Mercury extracted from solid metals</note>. However, this *Mercury* has specific qualities according to the nature of the *metal* or *mineral* from which it was extracted. For that reason—regarding our work, which is to dissolve perfect types <note>original: *Species*</note> of *metals*—it has no more power <note>original: virtue</note> than common quicksilver <note>original: *Argent Vive*; Latin for "living silver"</note>. 

There is, then, only one kind of liquid <note>original: humidity</note> that can be used in our *work*. It certainly does not come from lead <note>original: *Saturn*</note> or copper <note>original: *Venus*</note>, nor is it drawn from anything that nature has formed on its own. Rather, it comes from a substance compounded by the Art of the Philosopher.

He concludes that the necessary "liquid" for alchemical transformation is an artificial substance created by the philosopher, not a raw material found in nature.</summary>

So then, if a *Mercury* extracted from the [metallic] Bodies has not only the same lack of heat and excess of impurities <note>original: "faeces"</note> as **common** *Mercury* has, but also a distinct specialized form, it must (because of this form) be even further removed from our *Mercury* than common ***quicksilver*** <note>original: *Argent Vive*; Latin for "living silver"</note> is.

Our Art, therefore, is to combine two principles—one in which the *Salt* abounds, and another in which the *Sulphur* of Nature abounds—which are neither yet perfect, nor yet totally imperfect. Consequently, they may be changed or elevated <term>exalted</term> <note>In alchemy, to "exalt" a substance is to raise its purity or potency</note> by our Art, whereas that which is already totally perfect cannot be changed. Then, by using **common** *Mercury*, we extract not the physical weight <note>original: *Pondus*; Latin for "weight" or "bulk"</note>, but the heavenly virtue from the compound. This virtue, being like a ferment <note>original: Fermental</note>, produces in the common *Mercury* an offspring more noble than itself; this is our true *Hermaphrodite* <note>The "Hermaphrodite" or Rebis represents the union of male and female, or Sulphur and Mercury, into a single potent substance</note>, which will solidify itself and dissolve the [metallic] Bodies.

Observe a grain of corn <note>In this context, "corn" refers to a cereal grain such as wheat or barley</note>: in it, barely a visible part is the sprout. This sprout, if it were outside of the grain, would die in a moment. The whole grain is sown, yet the

### Sir George Ripley's Epistle

The sprout alone produces the plant; so it is in our **Body** <note>In alchemy, the "Body" usually refers to the solid metallic base being worked upon</note>: the **Fermental Spirit** <term>Fermental Spirit</term> <note>The active, life-giving essence that triggers the alchemical transformation, much like yeast in fermentation</note> within it is scarcely a third part of the whole. The rest is of no value, yet everything is joined in the composition. The impure <note>original: *faeculent*</note> solid parts of the Body are removed along with the dregs of the *Mercury*.

But beyond the example or analogy given of a grain of corn, it may be observed that the hidden and spiritual virtue of this, our **Body**, purges and purifies its "Matrix of water" in which it is sown. That is, it makes the water cast out a great quantity of filthy earth and a great deal of watery, salty moisture <note>original: "Hydropical Saline moisture"; "hydropical" refers to dropsy, an archaic term for an accumulation of excess fluid</note>.

For instance, perform your washings (as a trial) with pure and clean fountain water. First, weigh a pint of the water and record its exact weight. Then wash your compound eight or ten **Eagles** <note>An "Eagle" is an alchemical term for a single round of distillation or purification</note> (or times) and save all the residue <note>original: *faeces*</note>. Weigh your **Body** and *Mercury* exactly, and weigh your residue once it is very dry. Then distill or **sublime** <term>sublime</term> <note>To heat a substance until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid, a method used to separate pure substances from non-volatile impurities</note> everything that can be sublimed. A very little liquid *Mercury* will ascend. Then put the remaining residue in a **crucible** <note>A heat-resistant container used for melting or burning substances at very high temperatures</note>, set it on the coals, and all the impurities of the *Mercury* will burn like a coal, yet without

...a most pure *Sulphur*, and out of the other, the *Mercury of Mercury* (which is the kernel <note>original: Nut</note> of *Mercury*). Yet even with these, you could accomplish nothing, for the power of fermentation <term>Fermental virtue</term> <note>The inherent power of a substance to cause a chemical or alchemical change, similar to the way yeast causes bread to rise</note> is the wonder of the world. It is through this power that water becomes herbs, trees, and plants, fruits, flesh, blood, stones, minerals, and everything else. Seek for it alone, and rejoice in it as a treasure that is truly beyond price. 

Now understand that fermentation does not work outside of its own nature <note>original: out of kind</note>; neither do salts ferment metals. Do you wish to know why it is that some fixed alkalis <term>Alcalies</term> <note>Basic chemical substances, such as potash or soda, used in alchemical reactions</note> extract a *Mercury* out of minerals and more imperfect metals? Consider then that in all these substances <note>original: Bodies</note>, the *Sulphur* is not as deeply <note>original: radically</note> mixed and united as it is in *Silver* and *Gold*. 

Now, *Sulphur* is related to various alkalis, which are unusually easily dissolved or melted with it. By this means, the mercurial parts are separated <note>original: disjoyned</note>, and the quicksilver <term>Argent vive</term> <note>Latin: *argentum vivum*; the common alchemical name for liquid mercury</note> is separated by fire. The *Mercury* separated in this way is stripped of its *Sulphur*, when in truth, all that is required is a purification <term>depuration</term> <note>The process of clearing or purifying a substance of its impurities</note> of the *Sulphur* by separating the impure from the pure;

<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: but</meta>

<summary>George Ripley explains that "fermentation" is the essential force behind all natural growth and alchemical transformation, and discusses the difficulty of separating Mercury from Sulphur in precious metals compared to base minerals.</summary>
<keywords>Sulphur, Mercury of Mercury, Fermentation, Alkalies, Silver, Gold, Argent vive, depuration, George Ripley</keywords>

<header>An Exposition upon</header>

But these salts, having separated the *Sulphur*, leave the *Mercury* in a worse state—that is, more estranged from a metallic nature than it was before. In its original composition, that *Sulphur* of *Lead* <note>original: *Saturn*</note> will not burn; for even though it may be sublimed <term>Sublimed</term> <note>Heated into a vapor and then cooled back into a solid</note>, calcined <term>Calcined</term> <note>Reduced to a powder by heat</note>, turned into "Sugar of Lead," or vitrified <term>Vitrified</term> <note>Turned into glass</note>, it still returns to the same state it was in before when treated with fire and fluxes <term>Fluxes</term> <note>Substances used to promote melting</note>. 

However, once its *Sulphur* has been separated (as mentioned before), it will catch fire if joined with saltpeter <term>Salt-peter</term> <note>Potassium nitrate, a highly reactive oxidizing agent</note>, just as common *Sulphur* does. Thus, the salts act on the *Sulphur*—robbing the *Mercury* of it—but they do not act on the *Mercury* itself because they lack a ferment <term>Ferment</term> <note>A catalytic agent or "yeast" that triggers a transformation</note>. This ferment is not to be found except among homogeneous things <note>original: Homogeneal; things of the same kind or nature</note>. 

Therefore, the ferment of bread does not leaven a stone, nor does the ferment of any animal or vegetable substance ferment a metal or mineral. So then, even though you might obtain a *Mercury* out of gold by the help of the "Liquor of the First Essence of Salt" <term>first *Ens* of Salt</term> <note>Latin: *Primum Ens Salis*; a Paracelsian term for a liquid believed to hold the primary essence of salt</note>, that *Mercury* would never accomplish our Great Work. On the other hand, *Mercury* made out of gold by our specific *Mercury*—even if there are three parts of our *Mercury* to one of gold—

# Sir George Ripley's Epistle:

This *Mercury*, I say, will (through continual digestion <term>Digestion</term> <note>The process of exposing a substance to a gentle, consistent heat over a long period</note>) accomplish the whole work. Do not marvel, then, that our *Mercury* is more powerful when it is prepared by *Mercury*. For certainly the ferment <term>Ferment</term> <note>A substance that acts as a catalyst to trigger a transformation or "leavening" in the alchemical material</note> that comes between the compound body and the water causes a death and a regeneration; it does that which nothing else in the world can do. 

Besides, it separates from the *Mercury* an earthiness <note>original: "terrestreity"</note> which burns like a coal, and a watery fluid <note>original: "Hydropical humour"</note> that melts in common water. But the residue is activated <note>original: "acuated"</note> by a "Spirit of Life," which is our true embryonic <term>Embryonated</term> <note>Referring to something in its earliest, seed-like, or potential state</note> *Sulphur* of our water—not visible, yet working visibly. 

We conclude, then, that all operations to create our *Mercury* are erroneous and will never produce our Mystery, unless they use common *Mercury* and our "Body" according to our Art, no matter how wonderfully those other *Mercuries* may be made. For as the author of the *New Light* <note>Referring to Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), author of the influential alchemical text *Novum Lumen Chymicum*</note> says: *No water in any island of the philosophers was wholesome, except that which was drawn out of the kidneys <note>original: "reigns," referring to the Latin "renes," representing the loins or the vital source</note> of Gold <note>original: Sol</note> and Silver <note>original: Luna</note>.* Do you want to know what that means? *Mercury*, in its weight <note>original: *pondus*</note> and its inability to be burned, is volatile gold <note>original: "Gold fugitive"</note>...

From this ferment, even though it is a physical body, there proceeds a specific scent. Indeed, its **weight** <note>original Latin: "Pondus"</note> is often reduced if the compound is washed many times after it is already sufficiently clean. 

So then, the Ferment of **Sol** and **Luna** mediates in our composition. This ferment produces an offspring a thousand times more noble than itself. However, if you were to work on our compound body using the violent method of **Salts** <term>Salts</term> <note>Refers to harsh chemical reagents or mineral salts used in "vulgar" chemistry, which the author argues can ruin the delicate alchemical process</note>, you would end up with a **Mercury** far less noble than the original body. This is because the **Sulphur** of the body would be separated and not elevated <note>original: "exalted"</note> by such a process.

## STAVE XI.

*In the said book, the Philosopher also speaks—* <note>original: "The Philosopher"; likely referring to an authority such as Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus, whom alchemists frequently cited to authorize their work</note>
*If it please Your Highness to read it—*
*Of various Sulphurs, and especially of two,*
*And of two Mercuries joined to them indeed,*
*By which he leads those with true understanding*
*To the knowledge of the principles which are the only true ones,*
*Both Red, Moist, Pure, and White, as I have observed,*
*Which are, nevertheless, found by very few.*

We now come to the Third Conclusion: that among all metallic and mineral **Sulphurs** <term>Sulphurs</term> <note>In alchemical theory, Sulphur represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of a substance</note>, there are only two that belong to our Work. These two have their **Mercuries** <term>Mercuries</term> <note>Mercury represents the fluid, passive, and "feminine" principle</note> essentially united with them. This is the truth of our secrets, though we (to mislead the unwary) may seem to claim the contrary. For do not think that, because we suggest two ways <note>likely referring to the "Wet Path" and the "Dry Path," the two primary methods of achieving the Philosophers' Stone</note>, we really mean

This Gold is no other than that which the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> see, but they do not know the hidden Spirit that is within it. The Fourth Conclusion makes everything that was said before perfectly plain; namely, that these two *Sulphurs* are: one, the most pure **Red Sulphur of Gold**, and the other, the most pure, clean **White Mercury**.

The other is, in all its proportions, true *Mercury*, yet very clean, and carries its *Sulphur* within itself, though it is hidden under the form and fluidity <note>original: "fluxibility"</note> of *Mercury*.

...their own hopes, and then cast the blame upon the Art <note>The "Art" refers to the practice of alchemy</note>, when in truth it should only be blamed on <note>original: "imputed to"</note> their own folly in not understanding the true Philosophers.

I know many pitiful sophisters <note>False alchemists or those who use clever but flawed reasoning</note> who obsess over many "Stones"—Vegetable, Animal, and Mineral. Some even add to these the fiery, "Angelical," and "Paradoxical" Stone, which they call a "Wonder-working Essence." Because the goal they aim for is so grand, they also make the methods by which they would reach it seem equally grand. This is a double path: one way they call the *Wet Way* <note>original: "Via Humida"</note>, and the other they call the *Dry Way* <note>original: "Via Sicca"</note>, to use their own terminology. The latter they call the *Labyrinthine Path*, which they say is fit only for the great and powerful of the earth to walk. The other they call the *Daedalean Path* <note>Named after Daedalus, the mythical craftsman; here it suggests a clever or "crafty" shortcut</note>, an easy way of low cost for the poor of the world to attempt.

But this I know and can testify: there is only one way, and only one *regimen* <note>A specific set of instructions or a method for managing the alchemical process</note>. There are no more colors than those we describe, and anything we say or write to the contrary is only intended to deceive the unwary. For if everything in the world must have its proper causes, there cannot be more than one result <note>original: "end"</note>...

Therefore we declare <note>original: "protest"</note> and must again warn the reader that, in our former writings, we have concealed much because of the two ways we have hinted at <note>original: "insinuated"</note>, which we will briefly touch upon. 

There is one work of ours which is called the *Play of Children* <term>Latin: ludus puerorum; an alchemical stage where the process is said to become so simple it is like child's play</term> and the *Work of Women* <term>Latin: opus mulierum; a stage requiring only simple heat, traditionally compared to household tasks like washing or cooking</term>, and that is boiling <note>original: "Decoction"</note> by the fire. But our linear <note>original: "lineal"</note> boiling is an internal work which advances every day and hour and is distinct from that of outward heat; therefore, it is both invisible and impossible to feel <note>original: "insensible"</note>.

At the end of three weeks, the "Soul" of the *Mercury* ascends with the "Soul" of the dissolved *Gold*. These are folded in the "everlasting arms of *Venus*" <term>In alchemy, Venus often represents copper or a specific green stage of the process known as the "Green Lion"</term>, for in this season, the mixtures <note>original: "confections"</note> are all tinted with a pure green color. Our *Body* is then black, like a crow's beak <note>original: "Crows Bill"; this refers to the *nigredo* or "blackness" stage, signifying the death/putrefaction of the matter before rebirth</note>, for in this operation everything is turned to powder, blacker than the blackest thing imaginable.

We often use passages such as these when we speak of the preparation of our *Mercury*. We do this to deceive the simple-minded, and for no other purpose than to confuse our operations—speaking of one when we ought to speak of another. For if this *Art* were set down plainly, our operations would be looked upon with contempt even by the foolish. Therefore, believe me in this: because our works are truly natural, we take the liberty of confusing the "Philosophers' work" <note>The intentional, artificial work of the alchemist in the laboratory</note> with that which is purely "Nature's work," so that we might keep the simple-minded in ignorance concerning...

...concerning our true Vinegar <term>The "Philosophical Vinegar" is a potent solvent, not household vinegar, used to dissolve the "body" of the metals</term>. Since this vinegar is unknown to them, their labor is entirely wasted.

Let me then, in conclusion, say only this much: Take our "Body," which is Gold, and our *Mercury*, which has been energized <note>original: "acuated"</note> seven times through its union with our "Hermaphroditical body." This body is a *Chaos* <term>The "materia prima" or raw, disordered matter at the beginning of the alchemical process</term>, and it contains the brilliance of the Soul of the god *Mars* <note>In alchemical symbolism, Mars represents Iron</note>, held within the "Earth" and "Water" of *Saturn* <note>In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents Lead</note>. 

Mix these two in the specific weight <note>original: *Pondus*</note> that Nature requires. In this mixture, you possess our invisible fires; for within the "Water" (or the *Mercury*) there is an active *Sulphur* or mineral fire, and in the Gold there is a dead, passive, yet real *Sulphur*. 

Now, when that *Sulphur* of the Gold is stirred up and brought to life, a new fire is created between the "Fire of Nature" (which is in the Gold) and the "Fire against Nature" (which is in the *Mercury*). This fire is made partly of the one and partly of the other, for it partakes of both. By these two fires united into one, both Corruption (which is the breaking down or "Humiliation" of the matter) and Generation (which is its "Glorification" and Perfection) are achieved. 

Now, understand that God alone governs this path of the Internal Fire,

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of the "Internal Fire," describing its spiritual nature and its role in the perfection of the alchemical work.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>Sir George Ripley’s Epistle.</header>

Although mankind is ignorant of how this fire progresses, by using our reason to observe its effects, we are able to discern that it is hot. That is, we see that it performs the functions of heat, which is <term>Decoction</term> <note>original: "Decoction"; in alchemy, this refers to a slow, steady heating or "cooking" process used to refine and ripen the matter</note>. In this specific fire, there is no <term>Sublimation</term> <note>original: "Sublimation"; the process where a substance is heated into vapor and then condensed back into a solid to purify it</note>, because sublimation is a process of "exaltation"—the raising of a substance to a higher state. But this fire is itself the ultimate <term>Exaltation</term> <note>original: "Exaltation"; the process of increasing a substance's power or dignity to its highest potential</note>; it is perfection itself, and there is no progress possible beyond it.

Our entire work, then, is only to multiply this fire—that is, to circulate the "Body" (the material) for as long as it takes until the power of the <term>Sulphur</term> <note>In alchemical theory, Sulphur represents the active, fiery, and masculine principle within matter</note> is increased. Furthermore, this fire is an invisible spirit; therefore, it has no physical dimensions and is neither "above" nor "below." Instead, it exists everywhere within the sphere of activity of our matter inside the <term>Vessel</term> <note>The sealed glass container, such as a flask or alembic, where the alchemical transformation occurs</note>. 

So, although the visible, physical substance may rise and ascend (sublime) by the action of the physical heat, this spiritual power is always just as present in the material that settles at the bottom as it is in the material in the upper part of the vessel. For it is like the soul in the human body: it is everywhere at the same time, and yet it is not restricted or confined to any single part.

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

<summary>Sir George Ripley explains that the alchemical fire is an invisible, spiritual force that permeates the entire vessel, functioning like a soul to refine the matter through decoction and circulation.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemical Fire, Decoction, Sublimation, Exaltation, Sulphur, Spiritual Virtue, The Vessel, Soul and Body</keywords>

<header>An Exposition upon</header>

This is the basis of one of our <term>paradoxes</term> <note>original: "Sophisms"; here, it refers to a clever or philosophical argument that seems contradictory but reveals a deeper truth</note>, namely, when we say that in this true "Philosophical Fire" there is no <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: "Sublimation"; while the material might vaporize and rise, the author argues the spiritual "fire" within it is already perfect and does not "rise" in a physical sense.</note>. This is because the fire is the life, and life is a soul, which is not subject to the physical dimensions of bodies. It is for this reason that opening the glass vessel or allowing it to cool during the process kills the "life" or "fire" contained within this secret *Sulphur*, and yet, not a single grain of the physical matter is lost. 

Common "Elemental Fire" is something any child knows how to light and control. But it is only the true Philosopher <note>The alchemist</note> who is able to recognize the true inward fire. It is a marvelous thing that acts upon the body, yet is not actually a part of the physical body itself. Therefore, this fire is a <term>celestial virtue</term> <note>original: "Cœlestial Virtue"; a heavenly or spiritual power.</note>. It is uniform—meaning it remains always the same until the time of its operation is complete. Once it reaches perfection, it acts no longer; for every agent, once the purpose of its action has been achieved, comes to rest.

Remember, then, that when we speak of our fire which does not "sublime," you must not make a mistake and think that the moisture of the <term>compound</term> <note>original: "Compound"; the mixture of substances being transformed in the alchemical vessel.</note> which is within...

<summary>The author explains that the alchemical "fire" is a spiritual soul or heavenly power that acts on matter without being physical. He warns that opening or cooling the vessel kills this internal life, even if the material remains.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophical Fire, Sublimation, Sulphur, Elemental Fire, Celestial Virtue, Alchemical Vessel, Soul of Matter</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Internal Fire," explaining why it is described as not being subject to the physical process of sublimation and how its spiritual nature differs from common, physical fire.</meta>

The contents of the glass vessel should not be thought of as "sublimating" <note>original: "Sublime"; to turn from a solid into a vapor</note> in a simple physical way, for that process must occur continuously within. However, the true "fire" that does not rise as vapor is the <term>metalline love</term> <note>original: "Metalline love"; the internal chemical attraction or spiritual affinity between materials</note>, which is above, below, and in all places alike. 

Now, to conclude everything that has been said: learn and be well-advised about which materials you choose to work with, for "an evil crow lays an evil egg," as the proverb says. Let your "seed" <note>The starting chemical material</note> be pure, and your <term>matrix</term> <note>original: "Matrix"; the vessel or environment where the transformation occurs</note> also be pure; then you shall see a noble offspring. Let the external fire be maintained at such a level that our <term>confections</term> <note>original: "Confections"; the mixtures being transformed</note> may react and move to and fro continuously. In a few days, this will produce that which you most long for: the *Crow's Bill* <note>original: "Crows Bill"; a symbol for the "blackness" or *nigredo* stage, the first major step in the alchemical process</note>. Continue your <term>decoction</term> <note>original: "Decoction"; the process of heating or boiling the substance</note>, and in one hundred and thirty days you shall see the *White Dove* <note>original: "White Dove"; the *albedo* or "whiteness" stage, signaling purification</note>, and in ninety days more, the *Sparkling Cherubim* <note>original: "Sparkling Cherubim"; the final, exalted "redness" or perfection of the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

## STAVE XII.

*And these two things are best, he adds immediately,*
*For him who practices alchemy to take:*
*To make our gold and our silver as one with them,*
*Therefore I say, whoever wants to make our Pearl and Ruby <note>The "Pearl" and "Ruby" are symbols for the White Stone (producing silver) and the Red Stone (producing gold).</note>,*
*See that he does not forsake the mentioned principles:*
*For if the principles are true at the beginning;*
*And if he can also grasp them by skillful art,*
*In the end, he truly shall not regret his work.*

Thus we come to the final conclusion, which is that if a man’s operations are regular and his principles are true, his end will be certain—namely <note>original: "viz."; an abbreviation for the Latin *videlicet*</note>, the **Mastery** <note>original: "Mastery"; the successful completion of the Great Work or the Philosopher's Stone</note>. ...attempt to be foolish. Or if he should row at random with a ship gallantly furnished, he is no more likely to arrive at the golden coast than he is to stumble upon an unfortunate rock. Such fools are they who seek our secret in trivial matters and yet hope to find the Gold of *Ophir* <note>Ophir is a legendary biblical land famous for its wealth and high-quality gold.</note>.

*For a more exact guidance of your practice, take notice of these twenty rules following:*

### *Rule I.*

Whatever any deceiver <note>original: "Sophister"; historically, this referred to someone who used clever but fallacious arguments to mislead others</note> may suggest to you, or you may read in any deceptive <note>original: "Sophistical"</note> author, let no one move you from this fundamental ground: namely <note>original: "viz."; an abbreviation for the Latin *videlicet*</note>, that since the end result you look for is gold, let gold be the subject on which you work, and no other.

### *Rule II.*

Let no one deceive you by telling you that our *Gold* is not common gold, but "Philosophical Gold." It is true that common gold is "dead" <note>In alchemy, "dead" metals are those in their solid, stable state, lacking the "life" or "spirit" needed for transformation.</note>; however, as we prepare it, there is a "quickening" <note>original: "quickening"; the process of bringing something to life or making it biologically active</note> of it, just as a grain of corn <note>In Early Modern English, "corn" referred to any cereal grain or seed, not specifically maize.</note> is quickened in the earth.

So then in our work, after six weeks, gold that was dead becomes quick <note>original: "quick"; meaning alive or active</note>, living, and generative; and in our composition, it may be called *Our Gold*, because it is joined with an agent that will certainly bring it to life. In the same way, a condemned man is called a "dead man," even though he is currently living.

### *Rule III.*

Besides gold, which is the "body" or the male, you must have another seed <note>original: "Sperm"; in this context, it refers to the vital reproductive essence required for the alchemical process</note>, which is the spirit and soul, or the female; and this is *Mercury*. In its flow and form, it is like common quicksilver <note>original: "Argent Vive"; Latin for "living silver"</note>, yet it is cleaner and more pure.

There are many who, instead of *Mercury*, will use strange waters or liquids which they call by the name of *Philosophical Mercury*. Do not be deceived by them, for what a man sows, that is what he must expect to reap. If you sow your "body" <note>the gold</note> in any earth except that which is metallic and of the same nature <note>original: "Homogeneal"</note> as itself, you shall—instead of a *Metallic Elixir*—reap a worthless powder <note>original: "Calx"; the crumbly residue left after a metal has been burned or oxidized</note> that will be of no value.

### Rule IV.

Our Mercury is the same in substance as common **quicksilver** <note>original: "Argent Vive"; Latin for 'living silver'</note>, but it is very different in form. It possesses a celestial <note>original: "Coelestial"; referring to a refined, heavenly, or spiritual state</note>, fiery form and excellent power <note>original: "Virtue"</note>; this is the nature it receives through our laboratory preparation.

### Rule V.

The entire secret of our preparation is this: take that mineral which is most closely related to gold and to *Mercury*. Saturate <note>original: "Impregnate"</note> this with "volatile gold," which is found in the veins <note>original: "reins"; referring to the internal deposits or essence</note> of iron <note>original: "Mars"; the alchemical name for iron</note>. Use this to purify your *Mercury* until seven cycles <note>original: "seaven times"</note> have passed; then it is ready for the "King's Bath" <note>An alchemical term for the chemical bath or solvent used to dissolve the "King," which represents gold</note>.

### Rule VI.

Know, however, that repeating this process between seven and ten times makes the *Mercury* better and better, and more active. With each preparation, it is sharpened <note>original: "acuated"</note> by our true *Sulfur* <note>original: "Sulphur"</note>. If you exceed this number of preparations, the mercury becomes too fiery; instead of dissolving the "body" <note>the gold</note>, it will solidify <note>original: "Coagulate"</note> itself.

### Rule VII.

This *Mercury*, having been sharpened <note>original: "acuated"; meaning to make chemically active or sharp</note> in this way, is afterwards to be distilled in a **glass retort** <note>A flask with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note> two or three times. This is done because some small particles <note>original: "Atoms"</note> of the metal <note>original: "Body"; likely referring to the metallic impurities or the iron used in the previous steps</note> may remain in it, having been imperceptibly <note>original: "insensibly"</note> left behind during the preparation of the *Mercury*. Afterward, it must be cleaned thoroughly with **vinegar** and **sal-ammoniac** <note>original: "Sal-armoniack"; ammonium chloride, a salt used in alchemy for cleaning and as a flux</note>; then it is ready for the Work.

### Rule VIII.

Choose your **gold** for this work so that it is pure and free from any other metals <note>original: "mixture"</note>. If it is not pure when you buy it, purify it through refining <note>original: "Purgation"</note>. Then, let it be made into a fine form, either by filing it into dust, hammering it into thin sheets <note>original: "Malleating"</note>, burning it into a powder <note>original: "Calcining"</note> using acids <note>original: "Corrosives"</note>, or by any other method that makes it as refined <note>original: "subtile"</note> as possible.

### Rule IX.

Now begin your mixture. Take one ounce of the gold <note>original: "Body"</note> chosen and prepared as described, and two—or at most three—ounces of the *Mercury* that has been activated <note>original: "animated"</note> as taught above. Mix them together on a **marble slab** <note>original: "Marble"</note> which has been warmed (only as hot as hot water can make it). Grind both together until they are thoroughly blended <note>original: "incorporated"</note>; then wash the mixture

## *Rule X.*

Understand now that regardless of anything we might say out of <term>envy</term> <note>Alchemists often used the term "envy" to describe their practice of intentionally obscuring their writings to keep secrets from the unworthy</note>, our method is no other than this. We declare, and will continue to declare, that neither we nor any of the <term>ancients</term> knew any other way. It is impossible for our secret to be achieved by any other principles or any other arrangement than this. 

Our <term>intentional deception</term> <note>original: "Sophism"</note> lies only in the two kinds of <term>fire</term> used in our work. The <term>internal secret fire</term>, which is God's instrument, has no physical qualities that can be felt by humans. We often speak of that fire while appearing to speak of <term>external heat</term>, and this causes many errors among the unwary. This is our <term>fire</term> which is increased in stages <note>original: "graduated"</note>. 

As for the <term>external heat</term>, it remains almost constant <note>original: "linear"</note> throughout the process until the <term>white stage</term> <note>original: "white work"; also known as the Albedo, this is a major milestone in the alchemical process where the matter becomes pure and white</note> is reached. It stays the same without change, except that during the first seven days we keep the heat a little lower for the sake of certainty and safety, though an experienced <term>philosopher</term> <note>In this context, an alchemist</note> does not need to do this. 

But the <term>internal governing heat</term> is in-

### Rule XI.

Next, you must provide a <term>glass vessel</term> <note>original: "Glass Tun"</note> in which you can complete your work; without this, you could never achieve anything. ### Rule XII.

You must then provide yourself with a furnace, called an **athanor** <term>athanor</term> <note>original: "Atha-nor"; a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a steady, uniform temperature for long periods without constant attention</note> by wise men, in which you may complete your work. No ordinary furnace will serve for your first work; you need one where you can produce a dull red heat <note>original: "heat obscurely red"</note> at your will, or a lower heat, and which can maintain its highest level of heat for at least twelve hours.

To achieve this, observe these steps: First, ensure your **nest** <term>nest</term> <note>The internal shelf or compartment within the furnace where the heating vessel is placed</note> is no larger than necessary to hold your dish, with about an inch of space <note>original: "vacancy"</note> at the side where the vent-hole of your athanor is located, to allow the fire to circulate.

Second, let your dish be no larger than what is needed to hold one glass vessel, with about an inch of thickness for the **ashes** <term>ash bath</term> <note>Alchemists placed their glass vessels in a bed of sand or ashes within the dish to distribute heat evenly and protect the glass from direct flame</note> between the glass and the side of the dish. Remember the saying of the philosophers: "One Glass, One Thing, One Furnace" <note>A core alchemical principle stating that the Great Work requires a single vessel, a single base matter, and a single consistent heat source</note>. For such a dish, standing with

*Thirdly*, if your **dish** <term>dish</term> <note>original: "Dish" or "Platter"; the tray holding the ash or sand bath in which the glass vessel is placed</note> is larger, your furnace vent must be between a third and a fourth as large as the diameter of your platter; otherwise, it cannot be heated accurately or continuously.

*Fourthly*, if your **tower** <term>tower</term> <note>The vertical part of the athanor furnace, designed to hold a supply of charcoal that feeds the fire as it burns</note> is more than six inches square at the fire-place, your proportions are wrong, and you can never achieve the correct level of heat. For if you cause a tower larger than those proportions to stream with flame, the heat will be too intense; and if it does not stream, the heat will not be sufficient, or it will be achieved only with great difficulty.

*Fifthly*, let the top of your furnace be closed, except for a hole about three inches in diameter (or square) that is just large enough for adding coals. This will effectively contain and direct the heat.

### Rule XIII.

Once these things are arranged in this way, place your matter into your **glass** <term>glass</term> <note>The glass vessel or "philosopher's egg" used to contain the alchemical mixture</note> and apply fire as nature requires—gentle and not too violent. Begin at the stage where nature left off. Now, understand that nature has left your materials in the **mineral kingdom** <term>mineral kingdom</term> <note>Alchemists categorized the world into three kingdoms: Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral; each required different conditions for growth</note>. Therefore, although we draw comparisons from plants <note>original: "Vegetables"</note> and animals, you must understand a parallel within the specific kingdom in which the subject you are handling is placed.

For instance, if I were to make an analogy <note>original: "Analogize"</note> between the reproduction <note>original: "Generation"</note> of a human being and the growth <note>original: "Vegetation"</note> of a plant, you must not assume that the heat required for one is to be measured by the heat required for the other. We know that plants will grow in the ground, which is not without heat—the heat they feel in the earth even at the beginning of spring. However, an egg would not be hatched by that same level of heat, nor could a person feel any warmth from it; rather, to a human, it would feel like a numbing cold.

Since you know that your work belongs entirely to the mineral kingdom, you must understand what level of heat is appropriate for mineral bodies and what may truly be called a gentle heat,

<meta>catchword: heat,</meta>

<summary>This page explains that alchemical heat must be specifically calibrated for the mineral kingdom, using analogies to show that "gentle heat" varies significantly between plants, animals, and minerals.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Rule XIII, Mineral Kingdom, alchemical heat, analogy, generation, vegetation, glass vessel</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the precise regulation of heat. It explains that "gentle" heat in the mineral kingdom is much hotter than "gentle" heat in the vegetable or animal kingdoms.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>An Exposition upon</header>

heat and what is violent. First, now consider where nature leaves you—not only within the **mineral kingdom** <term>mineral kingdom</term> <note>One of the three traditional divisions of nature (animal, vegetable, mineral); alchemy primarily deals with minerals</note>, but specifically working within it on **gold** and ***mercury***, both of which are incombustible <term>incombustible</term> <note>A substance that cannot be consumed or destroyed by fire</note>. 

Yet *mercury*, being delicate <note>original: "tender"</note>, will break any vessel if the fire is too extreme. Therefore, although it is incombustible and no fire can hurt the substance itself, it must also be kept with the **male sperm** <term>male sperm</term> <note>In alchemy, this refers to the active, sulfurous principle or the "seed" of gold used to fertilize the matter</note> in one **glass** <term>glass</term> <note>The sealed alchemical vessel, often called the philosopher's egg</note>. If the fire is too high, this union cannot be maintained, and consequently, the work cannot be finished. 

So then, a temperate heat <term>temperate heat</term> <note>A moderated, steady heat appropriate for the specific stage of the alchemical process</note> ranges from the degree of heat that keeps **lead** or **tin** constantly melted up to the highest temperature the glass can endure without danger of breaking. You must begin your degrees of heat according to the kingdom in which nature has left you.

Just as the highest degree of heat that the root of a tree feels in the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth is not nearly comparable to the lowest degree of heat an animal possesses, so too the highest degree of heat a plant <note>original: "vegetable"</note> will endure without burning is far too low for the first degree of mineral heat required for our work.

<header>Sir George Ripley’s Epistle.</header>

### Rule XIV.

Understand that all your progress in this Work is to ascend in the clouds <note>original: *Bus & Nubi* and *in Nubibus*</note>, rising from the Moon <note>symbolizing Silver or the "cool" lunar principle</note> up to the Sun <note>symbolizing Gold or the "hot" solar principle</note>. Therefore, I command you to perform **sublimation** <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes, then allowing it to condense back into a solid</note> in a constant vapor, so that the Stone may breathe <note>original: "take Air"</note> and come to life.

### Rule XV.

This is not enough, however. To achieve our permanent **tincture** <term>tincture</term> <note>The final, potent alchemical substance used for transformation</note>, the "water of our lake" <note>a metaphorical term for the alchemical solvent or mercury</note> must be boiled with the ashes of **Hermes’ tree** <term>Hermes' tree</term> <note>A symbolic representation of the alchemical process's growth; its "ashes" are the solid residues after heating</note>. I command you then to boil it day and night without ceasing, so that amidst the "troubles of the stormy sea"—the violent motion within the vessel—the heavenly nature may rise and the earthly nature may sink.

Truly, if we did not boil the mixture, we would never call our work **decoction** <term>decoction</term> <note>The process of boiling a substance to extract its essence or concentrate it</note>, but rather **digestion** <term>digestion</term> <note>A gentler heating process that mimics the slow breakdown of food in a stomach</note>. For when the spirits only circulate silently and the compound at the bottom does not move by **ebullition** <term>ebullition</term> <note>the act of bubbling or boiling up</note>, the process is properly called only "digestion."

<meta>catchword: Rule XVI.</meta>

<summary>This page outlines two specific rules for the alchemical work: the necessity of constant sublimation to "animate" the Stone and the requirement of vigorous boiling (decoction) to separate the heavenly and earthly natures of the matter.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, Moon and Sun, Hermes' tree, tincture, decoction vs digestion, ebullition, George Ripley</keywords>

### *Rule XVI.*

Do not be too hasty, expecting the harvest too soon or the end shortly after the beginning. For if you are patient and the process is maintained <note>original: "patiently supported"</note>, within fifty days at the most, you will see the **Crow’s Bill** <term>Crow’s Bill</term> <note>An alchemical term for the appearance of a deep black color, signaling the stage of "nigredo" or putrefaction, which is the necessary first step of transformation.</note>.

Many (say the Philosophers) imagine our **solution** <term>solution</term> <note>The chemical process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid state.</note> to be an easy task; but only those who have tried it and gained experience can tell how difficult it truly is. Do you not see a grain of corn? Sow it, and after three days you will only see it swollen; if it were dried then, it would be the same corn it was before. Yet you cannot say it was not cast into its proper **womb** <term>matrix</term> <note>The environment or substance in which something is cast or formed; in this analogy, the soil.</note>, for the earth is its true place; it simply needed its proper time to **grow** <term>vegetate</term> <note>The natural process of plant growth; in alchemy, this refers to the "life" or growth of the metals.</note>.

But things with a harder kernel lie in the ground a much longer time, such as nuts and plum stones, for everything has its season. This is a true sign of a natural process: that it waits for its season and is not **rushed** <term>precipitate</term> <note>Acting with undue haste or occurring suddenly before the proper time.</note>. Do you think, then, that gold—the most solid substance in the world—will change its form in a short time? No, you must wait and wait until

<meta>This page ends mid-sentence on the necessity of patience when working with gold, leading into the next stage of the timeline.</meta>

<summary>Rule XVI emphasizes the necessity of patience in alchemy, comparing the slow transformation of gold to the natural growth cycles of seeds and nuts. It notes that the first sign of success, the "Crow's Bill" or black stage, should appear within fifty days.</summary>

### *Rule XVII.*

Beware that your fire does not go out, even for a moment, so as to let your matter grow cold; for if it does, the ruin of the work will certainly follow.

From what has been said, you may gather that all our work is nothing else but an incessant boiling of your compound in the first degree of liquefying heat <note>The lowest level of heat required to keep the substance in a state of fluid transformation without scorching it.</note> found in the metallic kingdom. In this state, the internal vapors shall circulate around your matter, and in that vapor <note>original: "fume"</note>, it shall both die and be revived.

### *Advertisement*

Although the rest of his **Adept Brethren** <term>Adept Brethren</term> <note>A term for the "Adepts" or masters of alchemy who claimed to have discovered the Philosopher's Stone and often formed secret intellectual circles.</note> had (as we might say, out of envy) sworn an oath of secrecy—contrary to their established principle <note>original: "Maxim"</note> of doing all the good they can with this great talent <note>A reference to the biblical "Parable of the Talents," suggesting that spiritual or scientific knowledge is a gift from God that must be used productively rather than hidden.</note> as long as they live, and even longer if possible—yet he had not sworn such an oath, even though they assumed he had. For, as he himself confesses, he felt an extraordinary inner impulse **to be helpful to all sincere searchers of this secret art** (to use his own words) and **to stretch out his hand to those who are trailing behind.**

Seeing, therefore, that it was the author's own desire to benefit the world through his labors, and that he gave his consent to **Mr. Starkey** <note>George Starkey (1628–1665) was an influential American-born chemist who acted as the primary editor and promoter for the works of the anonymous alchemist known as Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note> to print his writings (as appears in his *Preface to the Marrow of Alchemy*), I see no reason why his writings should remain hidden any longer. It was a great pity that Mr. Starkey separated this author's **Commentary upon Sir George Ripley’s Twelve Gates** <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was one of the most famous English alchemists; his "Twelve Gates" describes the twelve essential stages of the alchemical process.</note>. Starkey did this, as I was informed by someone to whom he gave the very book from which he admitted he had cut out the last six "gates" <note>The final six chapters or stages of the work.</note>. When this person asked him why he had cut them apart, he answered that the

## *Advertisement.*

[He felt the] world was unworthy of them; nevertheless, he promised to give that person a transcript of them, but he did not. This is why they cannot yet be found, a loss that is deeply regretted. Therefore, if any gentleman has them in his possession, or any other work by this author, it is humbly requested that they send them to the ***Pelican*** in ***Little Britain***, ***London*** <note>The "Pelican" was the name of a bookshop located in Little Britain, a street in London famous at the time for its many booksellers and publishers.</note>, so they may be printed with the first ***Six Gates*** <note>This refers to the first six chapters of the author's commentary on George Ripley's *The Compound of Alchemy*, which is structured around twelve "gates" or stages of the alchemical process.</note>, which are currently being printed. 

And so that I may do my part to help discover this author's works, I take the liberty of presenting the reader with a list <note>original: "Catalogue"</note> of the pieces known to be written by this author under the pseudonym <note>original: "disguised name"</note> ***Eirenaeus Philalethes*** <note>A famous pseudonym meaning "peaceful lover of truth." His true identity was a subject of great mystery, though modern scholarship often identifies him as the American-born alchemist George Starkey himself, or a persona he created.</note>. Some of these were listed by Mr. ***Starkey*** in his aforementioned preface, and others are mentioned by the author himself, along with several others which he says he wrote for his own enjoyment <note>original: "recreation"</note> and later burned. This author is universally acknowledged to be an Englishman and an **Adept** <term>Adept</term> <note>A master of alchemy who claimed to have successfully achieved the "Great Work"—the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. He is supposed to be still living and traveling, approximately 55 years old, though his true name is not known with certainty.

<meta>This page continues the argument that the alchemical process mirrors the divine creation, moving from the general concept of a "confused mass" to the specific requirement for unity and simplicity in the alchemist's work.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
# The Preface of Sir George Ripley

*All our secrets must spring from one image.*

Just as all things had an actual existence according to their various kinds from one mixed and confused mass, so all these secrets must flow from one image. Truth does not consist in diversity <note>original: "heterogeneity"</note>, but in unity; for God is one, and His works are uniform. The more noble any thing is, the closer it is to simplicity.

*As in the books of the philosophers, for whoever desires to see.*

The declarations <note>original: "sentences," meaning authoritative maxims or opinions</note> of the philosophers agree with this—at least as many as have truly understood the *Secret*—as *Morien* <note>Morienus, a legendary hermit and alchemist said to have taught the caliph Khalid ibn Yazid</note> often and abundantly testifies, along with *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational figure in alchemy</note>, *Trevisan* <note>Bernard Trevisan, a 15th-century Italian alchemist</note>, and many others. The substance is only of one kind, though it is two in number <note>This likely refers to the "two-in-one" nature of the alchemical Rebis, or the dual principles of sulfur and mercury</note>; and although more things are used, they are not fit to enter into this work until they are all brought to a oneness of nature.

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Our</meta>

<summary>Sir George Ripley emphasizes that alchemy, like God's creation, must begin from a single, unified source. He cites famous alchemical authorities like Morien, Geber, and Trevisan to support the idea that the "Secret" relies on reducing complex substances to a simple, unified nature.</summary>

## *Our Stone is called the Lesser World.*

And therefore our *Stone* is compared to Man, who—although he has a wife different from him in sex—is yet one with her in nature <note>original: "kind"</note>. In this sense, it is called the *Microcosm, or Lesser World* <note>From the Greek "mikros kosmos," meaning "little world"</note>. For indeed, next to Man, who is the Image of God, it is the true little system of the Great World. I shall not explain in detail <note>original: "particularize"</note> here how this is so, for it will be addressed in due time <note>original: "fall in seasonably"</note> in its proper place.

## *One and Three.*

<margin>✓</margin>

Within this water, besides its mercurial nature <note>original: "mercuriality"</note>, there is a spiritual seed of *Sulfur*, which is the secret fire. This is the *Trinity*; these are called the

### Concerning Sulphur and Mercury.

Nature works over many years and ages deep within the bowels of the Earth, processing <note>original: "decocting"—a term for heating or boiling a substance to ripen it or extract its essence</note> *Mercury* alone without any additions. Alchemy <note>original: "Art"</note>, however, in order to shorten the work, first impregnates <term>Mercury</term> <note>the philosophical principle of fluidity and volatility</note> with a spiritual seed of <term>Sulphur</term> <note>the philosophical principle of heat and combustion</note>. By this process, the Mercury becomes powerful enough to dissolve metals. The alchemist then adds mature *Sulphur*, which further speeds up the process. From these two "parents"—who share a single root—a noble, royal son is born. This is not merely gold, but our *Elixir* <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note>, which is ten thousand times more precious.

## Of *Sulfur* and *Mercury*.

Because Nature takes many years and ages in the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the Earth to mature <note>original: "decocting"—the alchemical process of refining or "cooking" a substance through steady heat</note> *mercury* by itself without any additives, the *alchemist's craft* <note>original: "Art"—the practice of alchemy</note>—to shorten the process—first impregnates *mercury* with a spiritual seed of *sulfur*. Through this, it becomes powerful enough to dissolve metals, and then the artist adds mature *sulfur* to it, which shortens the work even further. From these two "parents" sharing the same origin <note>original: "one Root"</note> is born a Noble Son of royal descent—which is not merely gold, but our *Elixir*, ten thousand times more precious.

### *Perfectly Proportioned by Nature.*

### *What is our Stone, etc.?*

They marvel at the strange <note>original: "uncouth"</note> difficulty of the task, and they can hardly decide how to judge what they read, since all the philosophers claim it is a very easy thing.

*For birds and fish bring it to us; every*
*man has it. And it is in every*
*place, in you, in me, etc.*

And indeed, the ancient wise men have written this, and they still write the same thing today: namely, that it is found even in a dunghill, according to Morienus <note>original: "Morien"—a legendary 7th-century alchemist</note>. Because the expense <note>original: "charge"</note> is so low, they all write about it extensively. Regarding the time and expense required, Artephius and Flamel <note>Nicolas Flamel, a famous historical figure associated with the discovery of the Philosopher's Stone</note> say it is merely "children’s play and women’s work." Therefore, one excellent philosopher, writing of this Great Work <note>original: "Mastery"</note>, titles his treatise *Children's Play* <note>original: "Ludus Puerorum"</note>.

<summary>This page addresses the contradiction between the perceived difficulty of alchemy and the claims of masters like Morienus and Flamel, who insist the Stone is made from common materials through an easy, inexpensive process.</summary>

Yet trust me, though the wise men write this and it is true, there is nonetheless something to be added to their judgment <note>original: "Sentence"—meaning an authoritative opinion or statement</note>, just as the author of *The New Light* <note>original: *Novum Lumen Chemicum*, a famous alchemical text by Michael Sendivogius</note> well observed. "*The Wise Man*," says *Sendivogius*, "finds it in a *dunghill*, but the *fool* cannot believe that it is in *gold*." 

For my part (through the great mercy of God to me, an unworthy and ungrateful creature), I know the Art to be true—and not only that, but also very easy. I wonder that men of such great talent have studied it for so long in vain. I am confident of only this: it is the gift of God. It depends not on the one who wills, nor on the one who runs, but on God who shows mercy <note>This is a paraphrase of the biblical verse Romans 9:16, a common sentiment among alchemists who believed the secret was a divine revelation</note>. To this I answer: it is indeed *Mercury*.

Yet trust me, though the wise men write this way and it is true, something must still be added to their statement, as the author of the *New Light* <note>original: "Novum Lumen"—referring to Michael Sendivogius’s influential 1604 work, *The New Chemical Light*</note> well observed: namely, that this Art is easy for the one who understands it, as *Artephius* <note>a famous, possibly legendary, medieval alchemist</note> plainly states; but to one who is ignorant of it, nothing could appear more difficult. *The Wise Man*, says *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a Polish alchemist and chemist</note>, *finds it in a dunghill, but the fool cannot believe that it is found in gold.* 

For my part (through the great mercy of God to me, an unworthy and unthankful creature), I know the Art to be true—and not only that, but very easy as well. I wonder that men of such great talent have studied it for so long in vain. I am certain of only this: it is a gift from God. It is *not based on human will or effort, but on God who shows mercy* <note>original: "not in him that willeth, nor in him that runneth, but in God that giveth mercy"—a reference to Romans 9:16</note>. In this regard, for the glory of God, I am bold enough to confess that I possess the Art and have witnessed Nature’s operations in these hidden secrets before my own

For our *Mercury* is essential and fundamental <note>original: "Radical"</note> to our Body, and partakes of the nature of it entirely. This is most internal to all things, because all things owe their beginning to Water.

*Yet wisely understand what it is, and where you should seek it.*

> *Otherwise, I advise you not to take this work in hand.*

But for the person who does not know this "Ocean" of ours—in which our "Water" has its ebb and flow <note>original: "flux and reflux"</note>—nor the "Fountain" from which he may draw this water for his use, let him stay away from this. For such a person, it is a most dangerous science, as he can only expect to lose money rather than gain any profit.

### *For philosophers flatter fools with fair speech.*

No one should expect to find helpful guidance in our books if they do not know the true <term>Matter</term> <note>Matter: The physical substance or "First Matter" used as the starting point for the alchemical work.</note>, nor the true "Keys" by which our Matter is brought out of darkness and into the light. Truly, though we write for the enlightenment of a <term>son of Art</term> <note>Son of Art: A term for a dedicated, initiated student of alchemy who approaches the work with the correct spiritual and intellectual mindset.</note>, we also write for the inevitable blinding of all those "owls and bats" who cannot behold the light of the Sun, nor endure the brilliance of our Moon <note>Sun and Moon: In alchemy, these often represent the two primary principles of Gold and Silver, or Sulfur and Mercury (the "King" and "Queen").</note>. To such people, we propose strange tricks that suit their base desires: to the greedy, an easy way that costs nothing and takes very little time; to the lazy scholars, a mere game that requires no tedious hard work; and to the impulsive, a confusing and endless series of distillations.

I am not merely commenting on him—since I am writing from my own experimental knowledge—but I intend this treatise to serve as a guide to that brilliant work of alchemy.
