### Sir G. Ripley’s First Gate

...and its qualities, between which there intervenes a Spirit of Life. This spirit carries them up and down like a wheel, which turns until it returns to the place from which it started. It then begins again and turns until it finds its rest, which is in the Fiery Cathedra <note>original: Cathedra; Latin for "throne" or "chair," here referring to the seat or ultimate state of the fire</note>, the reddest of the red. This is the great *Elixir* that commands all metals and brings them to the highest state of nature, which is gold itself—having attained a more than perfect <note>original: plusquam perfection</note> state through the marvelous cooperation of art and nature.

This gold is your base or foundation, the center to which all your operations return and in which they rest. These operations are nothing but circulations within their own kind, and these circulations are incessantly carried along by the never-ceasing action of the fire. Even a short pause would significantly delay the work, and letting the heat die out would extinguish the process beyond recovery. If anyone were to ask us what the natural operation of our Stone is, we would answer: it is the making of active natures passive, and passive natures active, by continual decoction <term>the process of boiling or heating a substance to extract its essence</term>. # An Exposition upon

...the Wheel <note>The "Wheel" or *Rota* is a common alchemical metaphor for the circular, repeating nature of the chemical processes</note> which lifts the Spirit and lowers the Body; the second has a contrary operation, for it makes the Body active and the Spirit passive. Therefore, Calcination <term>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to effect a change</term> hides the inner depths <note>original: "profundity"</note> of the Body, while Solution reveals <note>original: "discovereth"</note> them. 

This process is nothing more than boiling hard and dry bodies in our *Mercury* <term>Philosophical Mercury, the universal solvent sought by alchemists</term> over a suitable fire until they are dissolved and liquified. Then, the same fire makes them rise as vapor <note>original: "fly"</note>; as they rise, they condense and return in drops onto the Body, moistening it. This is Solution and Sublimation occurring together, because the Water circulating over the Body softens it; by repeatedly returning, it eventually brings the Body back to its own natural state of moisture. 

In this Resolution <term>the breaking down of a substance into its component parts</term>, according to *Artephius* <note>a legendary medieval alchemist traditionally credited with living over a thousand years through the use of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, the *Sun* <term>gold or the masculine principle</term> loses its color and is darkened, and the *Moon* <term>silver or the feminine principle</term> does not give her light. This happens because all things are returned to their state of confused Chaos, or "first matter" <term>materia prima; the original, formless substance from which all things were created</term>, in which the elements and their qualities are mixed together.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical processes of Calcination and Solution as a cyclical "Wheel" that breaks down physical matter into its original, chaotic "first matter." The author explains how heat and "Mercury" cause substances to dissolve and rise as vapor before condensing back into a liquid state.</summary>

# Sir George Ripley's Second Gate <note>The "Second Gate" refers to Solution, the second of the twelve stages of the alchemical process described by Ripley.</note>

> *They are one in gender, and in number two,*
> *Whose father is the Sun, and the Moon the mother;*
> *The mover is Mercury: these and no more are*
> *Our Magnesia <term>a term for the "first matter" or the crude substance of the Philosopher's Stone</term>, our Adrop <term>a term for the "lead" or body of the philosophers before it is purified</term>, and no other*
> *Things are here, but only sister and brother:*
> *That is to say, Agent and Patient,*
> *Sulphur and Mercury, both essential to our purpose.*

The cause of this is the homogeneity <term>the state of being all of the same kind; uniformity</term> of the matter, in which they agree in essence, along with the difference of sex between them. They exist in the glass <note>the alchemical flask or vessel</note> as male and female. 

In terms of maturity, one is more mature and consequently more active (namely, the *Sun* <term>the masculine, active principle, often associated with gold</term>, who is therefore the Father). The other is more crude <note>original: "crude"; meaning raw, unrefined, or immature</note> in comparison to the *Sun* and is therefore more passive: this is the *Moon* <term>the feminine, passive principle, often associated with silver or purified mercury</term>, who is therefore the Mother of our Stone. 

This Mother is our Mercury (which is called the *Moon* because of its significant difference from any other common mercury) together with its internal true *Sulphur*, which is hidden under its *mercurial* form.

### Sir George Ripley’s Second Gate

Circulations—and every circulation has three periods <note>original: "periods"; here meaning stages or phases of a cycle</note>—so now I tell you that these three periods are **Altitude** (height), **Latitude** (breadth), and **Profundity** (depth). Altitude and Profundity being united make Latitude, and so our "Wheel" is turned round. The Profundity is the water below, the Altitude is the vapor or waters above, and the union of these two is in a **Calx** <term>calx: the powdery metallic oxide or residue remaining after a substance has been heated or "calcined"</term>, which is Latitude. This is achieved by liquefaction, sublimation, and calcination: liquefaction dissolves and mixes <note>original: "confounds"</note>, sublimation makes a substance volatile, separates, and washes it, and calcination unites and fixes <term>fixes: in alchemy, to make a volatile or fluid substance stable and resistant to heat</term> the material.

> Know that your entrance shall be in the West,
> Your passage forward to the North if you do well,
> And there your lights will lose their light entirely <note>original: "each deal"</note>,
> For there you must remain for ninety nights,
> In the darkness of Purgatory without light.

You must begin in the West and in the Autumn, which is barren, for that is when crops are gathered; take, then, the gold, which is the harvest of nature’s works,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of managing heat in the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the physical setup of the furnace and glassware to prevent the vessel from breaking.</meta>

### Sir George Ripley's Second Gate

...too big, you might happen to seek your fortune in the ashes. <note>original: "seek thy fortune in the Ashes"; this is a common alchemical warning that if the fire is too hot, the glass vessel will shatter, leaving the "treasure" of the experiment ruined in the furnace ashes.</note>

Therefore, do not be immoderate in managing the heat; and for better security, do not let the neck of your glass vessel be less than a span <note>a span is approximately 9 inches</note> in length, but as much longer as you see fit. The longer it is for a <term>tyro</term> <note>original: "Tyro"; a beginner or novice learner</note>, the better he shall work and with more security. But the usual length we use is about 12 or 14 inches high. This height being established, arrange your furnace so as to let about 3 or 4 inches of the top of your glass protrude through the cover of your <term>athanor</term> <note>a furnace designed to provide a steady, consistent heat over a long period</note>. If you can touch or endure the heat of any part of that neck without being burned, do not fear your fire, but stew the matter without worry; provided your glass is strong, a quicker fire is actually better.

Yet know that your furnace must be suitable for the task, for do not believe that the ancient philosophers formerly used our modern art of furnace-making. Instead, they made them of brick or earth with earthen covers, which had holes for letting out part of the necks of their glasses, over which they put a cover which they could remove and set back on...

<summary>Sir George Ripley provides practical instructions on the dimensions of alchemical glassware and the management of furnace heat, advising beginners to use longer-necked vessels for safety.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, alchemy, athanor, furnace construction, laboratory equipment, glassware, heat management, tyro</keywords>

--- Page 316 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for furnace construction and heat regulation, explaining how earthen covers and clay sealants allow the alchemist to safely monitor the temperature by hand.</meta>

...back on at their pleasure. This earthen cover was not as reflective of heat as our iron covers are; the end of the glass vessel that protruded through the hole of the cover could be touched without any injury. By being able to endure that heat with their hands, they judged the intensity <note>original: "temperament"</note> of their heat. 

Therefore, in your furnace, ensure your cover or top is sealed <term>luted</term> <note>sealed with "lute," a paste of clay or other materials used to make joints airtight and heat-resistant</note> with good quality clay <note>original: "Loam"</note> everywhere, at least half an inch thick. In this way, you will be certain not to have an excessively scalding heat within the hollow of your furnace chamber <note>original: "concavity of thy Nest"</note>, which you would otherwise have; thus, you may control your fire as you wish. 

You do not need to cover the necks of your glass vessels that protrude. By doing this, you will see this teaching of *Ripley* proven true: your work will proceed very successfully, and you will always be able to tolerate touching your glass with your hand. This is the true meaning of all the philosophers <note>referring to the established alchemical authorities</note>—to provide a reliable rule so that you never exceed the proper temperature. That rule is: as long as you can bear to touch any part of your glass, provided your furnace chamber is covered and the ends of the glass necks protrude, you have not exceeded the heat.

# SEPARATION.

Having now completed two of the Twelve Gates, I have come to the third, which is **Separation**. This stage begins to look as if the substances <note>original: "Matters"</note> were destined to increase, and to begin to relate to one another. <meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical separation, clarifying that the process relies on natural condensation and distillation rather than mechanical filtering.</meta>

### Sir George Ripley's Third Gate

every time. Nor are our separations made by filter, or by a <term>tritorium</term> <note>original: per tritorium; a vessel used for separating liquids of different densities, similar to a separatory funnel</note>, as many imagine who do not know the nature of our work and therefore fall into such foolish fancies.

Nature, then, performs everything in our work. Like a skilled craftsman, she makes no confused mixtures but first causes the moisture to rise. Because it cannot escape, it condenses into drops and descends repeatedly until it finally begins to be sharpened <note>original: acuated; to make more chemically active or pungent</note> by the "body" below. For naturally, all homogeneous moisture that is <term>cohobated</term> <note>repeatedly distilled and poured back over the solid residue</note> over a solid substance with which it has an affinity is sharpened by it. 

Gold, then, is a "body" in which the active qualities of heat and dryness are greater than in *Mercury*. When the *Mercury* is cohobated over it, it begins to become slightly more fiery or hot. Then the vapors, which before were more watery, become more airy. Through continued <term>cohobation</term>, the water partakes more and more of the *solar* <note>original: Solary; relating to the qualities of the sun or gold</note> nature, until finally this heat or *sulphur* <note>In alchemy, "sulphur" often refers to the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance</note> impregnates the *mercury*, causing it to congeal into a new body.

This Body is an intermediate substance between the physical Body and the Water—a sludge <note>original: *Limus*</note>, a new Body, or "Adamic earth" <note>original: *Adamica terra*; in alchemy, this refers to the pure, primordial earth used to create the first human</note>. However, it is sufficiently stable to endure the heat required for this Work and to suffer all the pain and woe of this, our "Purgatory" <note>a metaphor for the intense heat of the furnace</note>, in which it remains for six weeks without producing fumes or vapor.

But as for the Spirit, that is a delicate thing. It is not able to endure the Fire but flies from it and remains in the uppermost part of the Glass vessel. Only as long as the fumes arise do the ascending vapors continue to meet with those that are already above, until at last, forming into large drops, they fall back down. When the fumes cease, as much of the Spirit as the hollow <note>original: "Concave"</note> of the Glass will hold without running down stays above until the entire process of reduction to ash <note>original: *Calcination*</note> is perfected. Then, they are drawn down by a magnetic power <note>original: *Magnetical virtue*</note>. So, here lies the entire mystery of the proportion of the Glass to the Matter: namely, that the vessel must be exactly the right size so that in its hollow space...

# An Exposition upon

Some have called it their sharp vinegar <note>original: "Vinegar"</note> because of its ability to dissolve substances; others have called it a bird, a goose, a pheasant, and many other such names.

But because it rises in the form of a wind or vapor, the philosophers have called it their vapor, their smoke, and their wind. For this reason, they command that the "porter" <note>The "porter" usually refers to the seal of the vessel or the person guarding the heat of the furnace</note> keep a diligent watch so that it does not fly away or evaporate, for that would ruin the Work.

This "water" then flies—meaning the more spiritual part of it—while the physical part remains below in the form of a moisture which bubbles and boils continually. The smoke in the "head" <note>The top of the distilling vessel or alembic</note> condenses and returns in drops upon the "body." By this means, the body of *Sol* <note>original: *Sol*; Latin for "Sun," representing gold in alchemy</note>, which is naturally very stable <note>original: "fixed"</note>, is surprisingly made volatile. It sends out its subtle, fiery soul through the evaporation of the water.

In this way, the dead body has a spirit of life infused into it and begins to be endowed with a living soul, which moves upward with the spirit and returns with the

...it is no longer a physical body, but a spirit. # Sir George Ripley’s Fourth Gate.

...on, I shall detail all our conjunctions. We have three specific conjunctions, all of which must be known by anyone who intends to complete this mastery <note>The "Mastery" or "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) refers to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

The first is "gross" <note>In this context, "gross" means physical, raw, or material, rather than spiritual or refined.</note>, which I touched upon before; it is the *amalgamation* of *Sol* <note>original: "Sol"; the alchemical name for Gold, representing the sun and the masculine principle.</note> with our *Mercury* <note>original: "Mercury"; quicksilver, representing the fluid, feminine principle.</note>. Because the mixture is made of two things, it is called a "diptative" conjunction; and the compound is now called *Rebis*—that is, "two-thing"—according to the verse: *The Rebis is a single thing made of two* <note>original Latin: "Res Rebis est Vina confecta." In alchemical literature, "Rebis" (from the Latin "res bina," meaning double matter) signifies the union of opposites.</note>.

In this mixture there are two natures: the one more active, which is the *Mercury*, and the other more passive, which is Gold. Note here that the reason the Mercury is more active than the Gold is because the moving virtue of *Sol* is "sealed"—that is, his sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur"; the internal heat or "soul" of a metal that gives it form and color.</note> is imprisoned. Otherwise, when dissolution <note>The process of melting or dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note> is achieved, *Sol* then becomes the most active, and *Mercury* becomes more passive. In that state, *Mercury* is like the feminine sperm; because it is more raw and tender, it is more quickly acted upon by the fire. *Sol*, the masculine sperm, does not feel the fire until it has been penetrated by the *Mercury*,

-># An Exposition upon<-

...the mystery will be accomplished with the blessing of God.

Remember now that the more Water you have, the more your Internal Fire <note>In alchemy, "internal fire" often refers to the inherent heat or chemical energy within the substance itself, as opposed to the "external fire" of the furnace.</note> ought to be to dry it up. Therefore, when you shall make the proportion of Water to the ***Sun*** <note>original: "Sun"; in alchemy, the Sun represents Gold.</note> three to one, remember that your number of Eagles <note>original: "Eagles"; in the works of Eirenaeus Philalethes and other alchemists, "Eagles" represent the number of distillations or purifications performed to "animate" the mercury.</note>, which is the proportion of your ***Mercury***, ought to be nine, or at most ten.

This is the highest activation <note>original: "Acuation"; the process of making a substance (usually the mercury) more chemically active or "sharp."</note> of the Water, which is best for such a proportion. As for seven Eagles, two to one is a very good proportion; in this way, you have proportionately three Eagles for every one part of Water which is added to the Body <note>In alchemy, the "Body" refers to the fixed metallic substance, typically gold, which is being dissolved.</note>. Some are so clever as to say that with four Eagles well cleansed, the Work may be performed, and then the proportion must be three parts of the Water to two parts of the Body, but the decoction <note>original: "decoction"; the process of heating or "cooking" the alchemical mixture over a long period.</note> must necessarily be longer. I have never yet tried it, knowing the previously mentioned proportions will do far better, and nine months' time is sufficient waiting for a Philosopher <note>The "Philosopher" here refers to a practitioner of the "Philosophical" art, or an alchemist.</note>. Any shorter way is and would be acceptable, but more tedious ways are very unwelcome.

# An Exposition upon

...understand it fully; pay close attention then to my teachings <note>original: "Doctrine"</note>. Mix your Water <term>Water: the liquid alchemical solvent, often referred to as "mercury of the philosophers"</term> with your Body <term>Body: the solid alchemical material, usually representing the "masculine" principle or a metallic base like gold or silver</term> in a proper quantity, and grind them together diligently. When you have mixed them, shut them up in your glass vessel carefully, and let them stand there until they reach complete perfection.

And after you have mixed them and set them to the heat, be sure you do not stir them, much less open them, or add anything to them, or take anything from them—regardless of what any other author might seem to advise. For if you act contrary to these teachings, you run an extreme risk of losing everything. 

For just as it is with prostitutes who, by lying with many men, rarely conceive a child, so it is if you join crude *Mercury* after your first Conjunction. I will not say that it is impossible, but it is very unlikely that you shall ever attain our Mastery <note>original: "Maastery"; the successful completion of the Great Work or the Philosopher's Stone</note>. # Sir George Ripley's Fourth Gate

...showers and dews shall be moistened, and by continual washing shall be cleansed. Then, by the good pleasure of God, the daylight shall spring forth; what was before dark shall now become clear, and what was the blackest of black shall now be made very white.

Strengthen your fire a little then; continue it prudently and with discretion until such time as your Stone becomes white, very clear, and bright—sparkling like a newly drawn <note>original: "new slipped"</note> sword—and by dryness is reduced to an impalpable powder <note>A powder so fine that its grains cannot be felt between the fingers.</note>.

Now you have come to the end of the "white" stage, and you have a perfect Stone. Although this is as yet of only small power <note>original: "virtue"</note>, you may now take it out and use it through Fermentation, Cibation <term>the process of "feeding" the stone with liquid nutrients to sustain its growth</term>, Imbibition <term>the process of the stone absorbing liquid until it is saturated</term>, or Multiplication <term>the process of increasing the quantity or the transmuting power of the stone</term>, and make it fit for projection <term>the final act of throwing the stone upon base metal to transmute it into silver or gold</term>. In this way, if you have only one ounce, you may soon have a thousand.

# The Fifth Gate Opened,

->Which is<-

## PUTREFACTION.

*Now we begin the Chapter of Putrefaction,*
*Without which pivotal point no Seed can multiply;*
*This must be achieved only by the continual action*
*Of heat within the moist Body, not by manual force;*
*For otherwise, Bodies cannot be transformed naturally.*

We have already passed through four Gates; since the first was opened, the others stand open at your will, so that you need only to enter. The course and method of the Philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers to the "Philosophers by Fire," or practitioners of alchemy who sought the secrets of nature.</note> now leads us to the Gate of Putrefaction—a horrible Gate, whose entrance is dark with *Cimmerian* night <note>original: "Cimmerian night"; a reference to a mythological people described by Homer as living in perpetual darkness. > *Happy are those who are able to possess the blackness.* <note>original: "Felices atrum quotquot habere queant." In alchemy, the "blackness" or *nigredo* is the first successful stage of the Great Work, signifying that the matter has begun to rot and transform.</note>

The cause of this death, corruption, or rottenness arises from the action of continuous heat. This heat comes not so much from the external fire of the <term>Athanor</term> <note>A furnace used by alchemists designed to maintain a uniform heat over long periods.</note> as from the compound within itself. In this process, the "Fire of the Water"—which is contrary to nature—opens the perfect Body through continuous grinding <note>original: "contrition"</note> and boiling <note>original: "decoction"</note>. This releases its *Sulphur* that was imprisoned <note>original: "incarcerate"</note>. This *Sulphur* is the Fire of Nature. Through the continuous interaction and reaction <note>original: "action and passion"</note> between these two internal forces, combined with the constant external heat, the entire compound is brought to a state of corruption. It is sometimes roasted by the external heat, which causes the moisture to rise <note>original: "sublime"</note>; this moisture then returns of its own accord, repeatedly wetting the Earth until, due to the heat, the Earth has completely absorbed the moisture. At that point, it dies.

...the bodies, and this apparent corruption, was merely the natural step toward a glorious regeneration. If the spirits had not returned to this union, one could only have expected them to evaporate <note>original: "Exhalation"</note>. Spiritual tinctures or essences cannot be destroyed by corruption even in many plants <note>original: "Vegetals"</note>, and much less so in the most perfect minerals and metals. Therefore, the union of the souls with the bodies clearly proves a hidden purity beneath the apparent rottenness, which will be raised to transcendent glory after purification.

At this point, they said that the spirit and soul which were above were the heavenly quintessence <term>the fifth element or purest essence of a substance</term>, and the body below was the earth. ### Sir George Ripley's Fifth Gate

*For when heat works temperately in moist bodies,*
*It generates blackness first of all, which is*
*The designated sign of natural conjunction,* <note>original: "kindly Conjunction." In this context, "kindly" means "natural" or "according to its kind."</note>
*And of true putrefaction; remember this,*
*For then you cannot fail to alter the matter perfectly.*
*And so, you must enter through the Gate of Blackness*
*To reach the light of Paradise in whiteness, if you wish to succeed.*

In the beginning of our work, therefore—through the cooperation of both internal and external heat and the coinciding moisture of the matter—our "Body" produces a blackness like pitch. This usually occurs within forty, or at most fifty, days.

This color plainly reveals that the "two Natures" <note>The "two Natures" typically refer to the opposing alchemical principles, such as Sulfur and Mercury, or the "Fixed" and the "Volatile."</note> are united. If they are united, they will certainly act upon one another, altering and changing each other from one thing to another and from state to state, until everything becomes one regenerated nature and substance, which is a new "Heavenly Body."

# An Exposition upon

But before this renovation <note>original: "Renovation"; a renewal or restoration to a better state.</note> can occur, the "old man" <note>original: "Old man"; a biblical metaphor (from Ephesians 4:22) used here alchemically to represent the original, unrefined, or "impure" state of the matter.</note> must necessarily be destroyed. That is, your first "body" must rot and be corrupted, losing its form so that it may be rewarded with a new form that is a thousand times more noble. Therefore, our work is not a forced or superficial process, but a natural and radical operation <note>original: "radical Operation"; a process that affects the very root (Latin: *radix*) or essence of the substance.</note>. In this process, our "natures" are perfectly altered; though each has completely lost what it was before, they become a third substance that is homogenous <note>original: "Homogeneal"; of the same kind or nature throughout.</note> with both of the previous ones, yet without changing their fundamental essence.

In this way, those who sow in tears shall reap in joy. He who goes out mourning, carrying precious seed <note>In an alchemical context, the "precious seed" is the starting material that must "die" in the earth of the flask to be reborn.</note>, shall return with an abundant harvest, his hands filled with sheaves and his mouth with praises for the Lord. Thus the chosen or redeemed of the Lord shall return with songs, and everlasting joy shall be upon their heads; sighing and sorrow shall fly away.

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical substance must undergo a total "death" or corruption of its original form (putrefaction) to be reborn as a more noble, unified substance. The author uses biblical metaphors of sowing and reaping to illustrate the transition from the "sorrow" of the black stage to the "joy" of the completed work.</summary>

### Sir George Ripley's Fifth Gate <note>In Ripley's *The Compound of Alchymy*, the Fifth Gate represents the process of Sublimation, where a substance is heated to a vapor and then condensed back into a solid to purify it.</note>

For it is a *labor* that can hardly be endured,
Because it contains so many tricks and turnings,
And he who tries it is surely lost, <note>original: "forlorne"</note>
Unless he is a skilled *Master*, believe me;
For I have tried both ways, yet could not see
How anyone following this path can be secure:
Therefore I, who have vowed secrecy,
Have written of this method—which we can barely endure
To attempt even for the sake of knowledge; its lack of ease will attract no one.

I have also hinted at our *Kingly* road,
Our way in which a fool can hardly err,
Our secret way, which will save much miserable toil,
Which is so easy that I may declare,
If you should see it, you would prefer it
To any earthly pleasure; yet beware
That you do not mistake one for the other, for I declare
That these lines present a mingled *doctrine*,
For both ways share a place in this *book* of mine.

->Learn<-

<meta>catchword: Learn</meta>

<summary>Sir George Ripley warns the reader about the extreme difficulty and deception found in some alchemical methods while contrasting them with a secret, "Kingly" road that is far simpler. He admits to intentionally mixing the descriptions of both paths within his text to test the seeker's discernment.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Fifth Gate, Sublimation, Kingly road, alchemical labor, secrecy, mingled doctrine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ripley's discussion of the "Fifth Gate" (Sublimation), focusing on the discernment required to distinguish between two different alchemical paths mentioned in the text: one laborious and one gentle.</meta>

# An Exposition upon

*Learn to distinguish every sentence well,*
*And know to which Work <term>Work: the alchemical process or "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> it pertains;*
*This is a great skill, which few, as far as I can tell,*
*Have ever been able to attain through all their reading,*
*And yet, this is the main point of the Theory:*
*Also, to know how to give accordingly*
*The proper heat; for in one method, you must be obliged <term>original: "fain"</term>*
*To increase the heat ten-fold—you may well believe me—*
*For what one process cooks <term>original: "decoct"; to boil or heat a substance to extract its essence</term>, the other will drive away.*

*Also, their operations appear*
*Different: the one you must sublime <term>sublime: the process of heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then condensing it back into a solid to purify it</term> and boil—*
*O tedious way! In which much time is spent,*
*And many errors occur, which will spoil the Work.*
*The other method silently requires no toil,*
*Like the "still, small voice" that came to Elijah <note>original: "Eliah"; a biblical reference to 1 Kings 19:12, where God's presence is found in a whisper rather than a fire or earthquake</note>;*
*In this Work, you do not need to burn or labor excessively,*
*Nor do you need the parching flame of fiery Vulcan <note>Vulcan: the Roman god of fire and metalworking, representing intense furnace heat in alchemy</note>;*
*A much more gentle heat begins and ends this Game <term>Game: a common alchemical metaphor for the experimental process</term>.*

<vocab>Theory, decoct, sublime, Eliah, Vulcan, gentle heat, Work</vocab>

<summary>Sir George Ripley advises the reader to carefully distinguish between two different alchemical methods mentioned in his writing: one that is tedious and requires intense heat, and another "gentle" way that requires far less toil. He emphasizes that understanding the correct application of heat is the most vital part of alchemical theory.</summary>

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Sublimation, alchemical heat, Vulcan, Elijah, decoction, the Great Work, discernment</keywords>

->Stanza 1. Position 1.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'W' set within a square frame featuring floral and foliage motifs.</image-desc>

When you have understood <note>original: wittily conceived</note> this properly, you may not labor in vain. From this, observe the truth and certainty of the Art; as Father Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical figure to whom the "Emerald Tablet" and the foundations of alchemy are attributed</note> says: "It is true, without falsehood, certain, and most true: That which is above is like that which is beneath; and that..."

*which is beneath is like that which is above*, to bring about the miracles of the one thing. <note>This is the famous "As above, so below" maxim from the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, suggesting a correspondence between the celestial and terrestrial worlds.</note> So Trevisan, Flamel, Denis Zachary, and others affirm based on their own experience. This is also stated by our author <note>Sir George Ripley</note> in his epistle to King Edward, in his conclusion of the "Admonition concerning Erroneous Experiments," and in other places within these *Twelve Gates*, so that I do not need to say more on this subject.

---

->Stanza 2. Position 2.<-

### Our Work is Made of Three Principles.

Where the *Red Man* and the *White Woman* are made one, and so forth. From this, it is evident that our operations are made of three principles, yet are of one essence: the *Red Man* <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Red Man often represents Sulfur, the fixed principle, or the "solar" aspect of the work.</note>, the *White Wife* <note>The White Woman or White Wife typically represents Mercury, the volatile principle, or the "lunar" aspect.</note>, and the *Spirit of Life*. By the latter, the first two are joined in marriage <note>original: espoused</note> or made into one. This is what Trevisan calls his "One Root" and "Two Mercurial Substances," which are raw when they are taken and extracted from their minerals <note>original: Minera's</note>. Elsewhere, our author calls this his *Trinity* and *Unity*; the *Trinity* refers to the substances as they are separate <note>original: severall</note>;

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of the Three Principles—symbolized as the Red Man, the White Woman, and the Spirit of Life—which must be unified into a single essence. It cites authorities like Trevisan and Flamel to support Ripley's teachings in the "Twelve Gates."</summary>
<keywords>Red Man, White Woman, Spirit of Life, Three Principles, Bernard Trevisan, Nicolas Flamel, Denis Zachary, Trinity and Unity, George Ripley, Alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the unity of the three alchemical principles, explaining how they share a single homogeneous essence despite their different manifestations.</meta>

...the *Unity* regarding their essence, which is entirely homogeneous <note>original: Homogenial; consisting of parts all of the same kind</note>. Therefore, it is added that they live in love and rest without conflict <note>original: repugnancy</note>, which could not be the case if they were not essentially and fundamentally the same. For likeness of nature is the cause of love, and oneness of essence is the true ground of union; among different substances, one can only expect confusion, if not destruction.

The *White Woman*, or female, is otherwise called the *Moon* by all philosophers; and by this

### Position IV.

*From an equal weight <term>original: Pondus; weight or measure</term> of Earth and Water,*
*Three parts of Water to one part of Earth is*
*good, but equal parts are best.*

Then make the Mercury <note>The "Spirit" or liquid solvent in the work</note> four parts to the Sun <note>Gold</note>, two parts to the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and so on, as it should be represented in the figure of the Trinity. And so we come to notice the doctrine of proportion between the Earth and Water: equal weight is best. Our author says the same in his chapter on *Calcination* <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a fine powder or ash</term>. *This is the surest and best proportion,* speaking of an equal weight of Earth and Water; and he gives the reason that solution <term>solution: the process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid</term> will be achieved sooner, namely:

*The more Earth you have, and the less Water there be,*
*The sooner and better the solution you shall see.*

--- Page 534 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of proportions in alchemical work, specifically explaining how the ratio of liquid (water) to solid (earth) affects the speed and success of calcination and the "reviving" of substances.</meta>

->A Breviary of Alchemy.<-

...have room to be sufficiently spread out <note>original: dilated</note> in the *Water*, and the "Body" (the solid matter) opened by it. This is the weight <term>original: Pondus; weight or proportion</term> recommended by *Roger Bacon* <note>A 13th-century English philosopher and friar often credited with early alchemical and scientific discoveries</note>, which requires a longer time before the "quick" <note>the "living" or volatile spirit, often mercury</note> is "killed" <note>stabilized or fixed into a solid state</note>. Consequently, the reviving of the "dead" <note>the solid matter being restored to a liquid or spiritual state</note> must take longer to complete. For *Calcination* <term>the process of heating a substance until it is reduced to a fine powder</term> is nothing other than "killing" the moist with the dry; until that is finished, there can be no reviving of the dry by the moist. Both processes happen during the same operation and period of time: as one dies, the other revives. The *Dragon* <note>an alchemical symbol often representing mercury or a volatile substance</note> does not die except with its sister <note>often representing sulfur or the fixed substance</note>.

---

->Position 5<-

->*The White Wife in the first Conjunction is to be three parts to every one part of the Red Man.*<-

Take three parts of the *Wife* and one part of the *Man*, and so on. Having looked at the weight <note>original: Pondus</note> between the *Earth* and *Water*, we now come to consider the proportion between the *Man* and his *Wife*. Here the weight is set at three to one, meaning there are four parts of *Earth* to four parts of *Water*, or potentially more, up to twelve; that is, three parts of *Water* for every one part of *Earth*. This also is

--- Page 535 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical proportions, moving from the ratio of water to earth to the specific "marriage" between the masculine (Sun/Gold) and feminine (Moon/Silver) principles.</meta>

### A Breviary of Alchemy.

...is clear from the chapter on *Conjunction*, where the *Woman* is assigned 15 veins to the *Man's* 5, regarding their fertility <note>original: fœcundity</note>. This is interpreted by the author himself as the first *Conjunction*: the *Man* must have only 3 parts of *Water* and his *Wife* 9, which totals 12 parts of *Water* to 4 parts of the *Earth*. By this, it is evident that the *Woman* should exceed her husband in a three-fold proportion.

Others suggest a ratio of *Two to One* following *Raymond* <note>Raymond Lull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan philosopher and reputed alchemist</note>, or *Four to One* according to *Alanus* <note>Alain de Lille (c. 1128–1202), a French theologian and poet associated with alchemical texts</note>; however, *Three to One* is considered best.

Nevertheless, in *Raymond's* "Doctrine of Proportions"—cited by our author in his section on the *Gate of Calcination*—one part of the *Sun* <note>representing Gold</note> is joined with two parts of the *Moon* <note>representing Silver</note>, which together make three parts of the "Body" (the solid material). To these are added four parts of *Mercury* <note>representing the liquid or spiritual essence</note>, which results in one more part of the spiritual than of the physical part. The author compares this to the *Trinity* <note>the Christian concept of Three-in-One</note> and *Unity*; both are considered good ratios. Indeed, *Alanus* prescribes four parts to one, which can be done, but three to one is best to achieve an equal weight <term>original: Pondus; the specific weight or proportion of ingredients</term> of spirit and life. This balance is necessary for completing the "Marriage" between this Royal Pair: the *Sun* (the husband) and the *Moon* (the wife). On this subject, the author says:

<summary>This page defines the ideal mathematical ratios for alchemical "conjunction," specifically recommending a 3:1 ratio of the feminine (liquid/spirit) to the masculine (solid/body) elements. It compares the proportions used by historical authorities like Raymond Lull and Alanus to explain the symbolic marriage of the Sun and Moon.</summary>

<keywords>conjunction, proportions, Raymond Lull, Alanus, Sun and Moon, alchemy marriage, gold and silver, Mercury, Pondus</keywords>

--- Page 536 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical "Marriage" between the Sun and the Moon, clarifying the symbolic language used to describe the ingredients of the Great Work.</meta>

### A Breviary of Alchemy.

...dy, <note>This catchword "dy" completes the word "Body" from the previous page.</note> as the author states in his *Gate of Solution* <note>The "Gate of Solution" is the second chapter of George Ripley’s "The Compound of Alchymy" (1471)</note>. They are one in nature <note>original: Gender</note>, but not in number. The Father is the *Sun*, the Mother is the *Moon*, and the mover <note>the active agent or catalyst</note> is *Mercury*.

This compound, depending on how it is considered, has many relationships and just as many names <note>original: denominations</note>: *Sun* and *Moon*, *Husband* <note>original: Man</note> and *Wife*, *Body*, *Soul*, and *Spirit*, *Earth* and *Water*, *Sister* and *Brother*, *Mother* and *Son*, along with many others. However, its proper name is *Magnesia* <term>Magnesia: In alchemy, this is not the chemical magnesium, but a term for the "Prime Matter" or the mystical substance from which the Philosopher's Stone is made</term>.

*Question: What is the Red Man? What is his White Wife? What is the Spirit of Life?*

It may be asked here: what is this *Red Man*? What is his *White Wife*? And what is the *Spirit of Life*? For these terms are the primary difficulty <note>original: knot</note> in understanding the writings of the philosophers <note>alchemists</note>. Their various expressions and seeming contradictions in this matter obscure the Art of alchemy wonderfully. Yet, however much they seem to differ in their writings, they all mean the same thing if they are rightly understood.

*Answer 1: What is the Red Man?*

The *Red Man* signifies the perfect body of the *Sun* <note>Gold</note>, or his shadow, the *Moon* <note>Silver</note>. For *Luna* <note>original: Lune</note>, the body—which is one of the seven metals—is a male and a perfect body.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic terminology of alchemy, defining the "Red Man" and "White Wife" as the Sun (Gold) and Moon (Silver), which together form the substance known as Magnesia.</summary>
<keywords>Magnesia, Red Man, White Wife, Spirit of Life, Sun and Moon, Mercury, Gate of Solution, George Ripley, alchemical symbolism</keywords>

-># A Breviary of Alchemy.<-

Because they married two males together; such are they who mix *Sol* <note>original: "Sol"; the alchemical name for Gold</note> and *Lune* <note>original: "Lune"; the alchemical name for Silver</note>, both in their physical and stable <note>original: "fixt"</note> forms together, whom the Spirit will never revive because there is no marriage-like love. Join therefore *Gabritius* <note>An allegorical figure representing the active, masculine principle or sulfur</note> to his beloved sister *Beya* <note>An allegorical figure representing the passive, feminine principle or mercury</note>, who is a tender maiden, and straightway *Gabritius* will die; that is, he will lose what he was. And from that place where he appeared to have lost what he was, he shall appear as what he was not before.

**Answer 3. What is the Spirit of Life?**

Thirdly, the *Spirit of Life* is *Mercury*. The "Mover," says this author, is *Mercury*, with which the Stone is to be multiplied <note>To "multiply" is to increase the quantity or potency of the Philosopher's Stone</note> once it is made. And it must be true *mineral mercury* without any foreign mixture, as *Arnold* <note>Arnold of Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> resolves expressly in his answer to *Boniface* <note>Refers to the "Letter to Pope Boniface VIII" attributed to Arnold</note>. And so *Ripley* <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a prominent English alchemist and author of "The Compound of Alchemy"</note> says some can multiply *mercury* with *Saturn* <note>original: "Saturn"; the alchemical name for Lead</note> and other substances, which we reject <note>original: "defie"</note>. Distill it, therefore, until it is clean, etc. Moreover, it must have all the proportions of *mercury*: its weight <note>original: "ponderosity"</note>, otherwise it could not be metallic; its moisture <note>original: "humidity"</note>, otherwise the feminine seed <note>original: "sperm"</note> would be lacking; and its dryness <note>original: "siccity"</note>, so as not to wet the

...yellowish, which colors show that the Matter begins to decay into atoms; this decay is not completed in less than 150 days (so as to let the *Sun* appear with its rays). ### Position VII.

*The East, denoting Whiteness, is the beginning of the Stone’s altitude.*

From there, through many colors, one ascends into the East, etc. In the Work <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) is the traditional term for the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> there are three dimensions: altitude, latitude, and profundity <note>original: "Altitude, Latitude, and Profundity" — here meaning height, width, and depth</note>.

The **altitude** is the perfection of the metallic bodies, which begins in "Whiteness" and is completed in "Redness." The **profundity** is the "First Matter" <term>First Matter: the primal, formless base material required to begin the alchemical transformation</term> into which they are resolved for the sake of multiplication <term>Multiplication: the stage where the power and quantity of the alchemical transmuting agent is increased</term>. The **latitude** is the means through which the Matter passes as it is lowered from its perfection, and from that debasement, raised to its glorification.

In this passage are infinite vivid <note>original: "gay"</note> colors, like...

-># A Breviary of Alchemy<-

<page-num>21</page-num>
<sig>B 4</sig>

...like those that appeared before Blackness, but more glorious. Note that the Stone has only three primary colors: Black, White, and Red. When the first stage (Blackness) is completed, it remains for three or four days at most; the second (Whiteness) lasts just as long; but in the last stage (Redness), the Stone remains forever. 

Between these periods, depending on whether the Matter is moister or drier, purer or more impure, many intermediate colors appear—more than can be numbered. However, two colors—namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet)</note> Green and Yellow—last for a long time before the Whiteness and Redness are achieved. 

Many colors also appear between the beginning of the Work <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) is the traditional term for the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> and the first appearance of Blackness. Although several colors appear then, they are dark, foggy, and foul-colored. This shows that Blackness is the dominant state, which for a time will appear like the "Egyptian darkness" <note>original: "Ægyptian darkness" — a reference to the biblical plague of thick darkness in Exodus 10:21; in alchemy, this represents the *nigredo* or putrefaction stage</note> and lasts for about the same duration. 

Similarly, although infinite colors appear between Blackness and Whiteness, their foundation <note>original: "Basis"</note> is Whiteness; therefore, they are bright and very glorious. Because these colors are only temporary, they pass away and others take their place until the Whiteness is perfected.

->Position VIII<-

->*The South, or Redness, is the completion of this Mastery.*<-

In this stage of the South <note>In alchemical symbolism, the South represents the highest heat and the final stage of the work.</note>, the Sun achieves completion <note>original: "Consummation"</note>. After the White stage, as the fire is continued, the compound will become azure, gray, and then citrine <note>Citrine: a pale yellow or lemon-gold color</note>, which will last for a long time; at last, it will end in a bloody redness.

->Position IX<-

->*He who thinks his Work is ended once the Stone has reached redness is mistaken.*<-

But turn your wheel <note>The "Wheel" refers to the cyclic repetition of alchemical processes like distillation and condensation, often called the "Wheel of Philosophy."</note> two more times, etc. Once the Stone has been brought to this state through constant and long decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating or boiling a substance to extract its essence or change its properties</term>, anyone who thinks the race is finished is miscalculating <note>original: "reckons without his Host" — a traditional idiom meaning to make plans without considering the person who has the most influence or the final bill.</note> and must reconsider. It is currently a Medicine of the First Order, and it must be brought to the Third Order through imbibitions <term>imbibition: the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</term> and cibation <term>cibation: the "feeding" of the dry matter with liquid to sustain its development</term>. This represents a second turning of the wheel; and by fermen-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the final preparation of the Philosophers' Stone, specifically focusing on the process of "fermentation" which follows the initial creation of the red substance.</meta>

-># A Breviary of Alchemy<-

<page-num>23</page-num>

Fermentation is the third turning of the Wheel <note>The "Wheel" refers to the repeating cycles of the alchemical process, such as distillation and condensation.</note>, which brings the Medicine to the Third Order. This stage makes it fit for Projection <term>Projection: the final step where the Stone is cast onto base metal to transmute it into gold or silver</term>, which it is not ready for at first. Until the Medicine can flow like wax, it cannot penetrate *mercury* before the mercury evaporates <note>original: "before its flight"</note>. The powder, as it is first made, is like grains or atoms and is solidified <note>original: "congealed"</note> in a heat far greater than that which makes mercury <note>The symbol ☿ represents mercury.</note> turn to vapor. Yet, it remains in the form of dust or powder, which must be changed before it is ready for Projection. 

Therefore, the Stone tints <note>original: "tingeth" — meaning to impart color and quality, thereby transmuting the metal</note> *mercury* into a metallic mass in the blink of an eye, as our author says in his preface, just as the *basilisk* <note>A mythical serpent said to kill with a single glance.</note> kills by sight. But the Red *Sulphur* <note>In alchemy, Sulphur represents the active, solar, or masculine principle of the Stone.</note> converts *mercury* into its own nature (namely, a powder) through a period of digestion, provided it is joined in the correct proportion and heated properly. 

Therefore, our author says that if you give it too much, it must be given a "vomit," or it will remain "sick" for too long <note>This is an alchemical metaphor for over-saturating the substance, requiring a corrective process to restore balance.</note>. However, the Stone will never let go of any *mercury* that has been joined to it through heat. Our *Sulphur* is then like a "Royal Infant" that feels both hunger and thirst; if you can act as a nurse to it as you ought, it will repay

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the different "orders" or stages of the alchemical Medicine. It explains how the final product (the Third Order) differs from the initial stage (the First Order) in its physical behavior and its interaction with mercury.</meta>

-># A Breviary of Alchemy<-

<page-num>24</page-num>

...repay both your efforts and your costs. Do not stop where you should actually begin; instead, continue until you bring the substance to the Third Order, which Raymond Lully <note>original: "Reymond" — referring to the 13th-century philosopher and alchemist often associated with alchemical writings.</note> calls his "Oils and Ointments," and our author does the same.

There are three properties in which the White and Red *Sulphurs* <note>In alchemy, "Sulphur" represents the active, solar, or masculine principle of the Stone; White Sulphur is used for making silver, and Red for gold.</note> of the First Order differ from those of the Third Order:

1. One flows as easily as wax when heated or placed on hot metal; the other remains a powder even in intense heat.

2. One is like glass—brittle, heavy <note>original: "ponderous"</note>, and shining; the other is a powder as fine as atoms.

3. One enters *mercury* like an oil and solidifies <note>original: "Coagulates"</note> it in an instant. The other only absorbs the *mercury*, just as a calx <term>calx: the powdery oxide or substance left after a metal has been roasted or "calcined"</term> of a metal would do. However, it will not hold onto the mercury if the fire is increased too strongly, nor will it turn it into metal. But if the heat and the proportions are exactly as they should be, then through a period of digestion <note>A process of slow, steady heating in a sealed vessel to mature the substance.</note>, it transforms the mercury into its own nature. 

Thus, as George Ripley <note>original: "Ripley" — a famous 15th-century English alchemist and author of *The Compound of Alchemy*.</note> truly says, you may multiply both the White and the Red substances using *mercury*. Even if you did not have enough to fill a spoon at first, in a short time you may have...

It explains that the final medicine must be oil-like and capable of instantly solidifying mercury, while also describing the process of "multiplication" to increase the quantity of the Stone.</summary>

### Position 10.

### *Our Stone must have a specific affinity for metals before it can perform Projection.*

<note>Projection is the final stage of alchemy where a small amount of the Stone is thrown onto molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note>

**O**ur *Red Man* does not tinge <note>original: "teyneth" — to dye, color, or transform the nature of a substance.</note> until he himself is tinged, etc. Our *Red Man* or *King* <note>The "Red Man" or "King" is a symbolic name for the Red Stone or the gold-producing principle.</note> must be tinged by a ferment before it will transform imperfect metals. The ferments are only *Sol* <note>original: "Sol" — Latin for "Sun," representing gold.</note> and *Luna* <note>original: "Luna" — Latin for "Moon," representing silver.</note>. 

Let the proportion be one part *Sulphurs* <note>The active, masculine principle of the Stone.</note> to three parts *Sol* or *Luna* (depending on which Sulphur you are using), making the Sulphur a fourth part of the total compound. Alternatively, use four parts *Sol* or *Luna* and one part *Sulphur* (making the Sulphur a fifth part). 

Then, digest and putrefy <term>putrefy: a stage in alchemy where the substance is broken down by heat, often turning black, to allow for new growth.</term> your ferment with *Mercury*. Congeal it, and ferment it again until it flows like **wax** or **oil**. Then that **oil** will fix *Mercury* <term>fix: to turn a volatile or liquid substance (like quicksilver) into a solid that can endure fire without evaporating.</term> and turn any metal into perfection. 

You may then multiply it as you wish, or you may multiply it before fermentation. Then, take the perfect Stone to serve as your "body," and mix him with the *White Wife* <note>The "White Wife" refers to the purified feminine or lunar principle (mercury/silver) used in the alchemical "marriage."</note> in the same proportion as at first, and add the *Spirit of Life*...

<summary>This page explains the process of "fermentation," where the alchemical Stone is combined with gold or silver to give it the specific power to transform other metals.</summary>

...as at first, and digest it until it passes through the three colors: black, white, and red.

In this way, our author also concludes his *Erroneous Experiments* <note>The author is referring to a section of George Ripley's famous alchemical poem, *The Compound of Alchymy*, where Ripley warns against false paths.</note>: *“I never saw true work but one,”* he says. He did achieve one, but it was after infinite errors; indeed, no philosopher has ever seen any other work, which he briefly describes here.

*“Remember Man, the most noble creature, etc.”*—that is to say, gold. It is an error to write, “Remember that man is the most noble creature of earthly composition,” because man is not of earthly composition in this sense, whereas stones, metals, and clays are. Now, because we seek the noblest of creatures of earthly composition, we must be wise enough to take it as our principle. For as he says elsewhere: *“Just as fire is the principle of burning, so the principle of gilding is gold, I know <note>original: "I wis" — an archaic term meaning "certainly" or "indeed."</note>.”* 

In this “noblest creature,” he says, the four elements are proportioned by nature, which makes it incombustible <term>incombustible: in alchemy, this refers to a substance's ability to withstand the heat of a furnace without evaporating or being destroyed.</term>. If any one element were dominant, it would not endure the fire; but as Trevisan <note>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist known for his search for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> says, the analytical proportioning of the elements in a metallic substance is the very form of gold—or rather, that which gives gold its form. He adds

# THE
# VISION
### OF
## Sir George Ripley,
### Canon of *Bridlington*, Unfolded.
<note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was a famous English alchemist and Augustinian canon at Bridlington Priory. His "Vision" is a highly symbolic poem describing the "Great Work" of alchemy—the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

When I was busy with my book on a certain night,
This vision expressed here appeared to my dimmed sight:
I saw a very reddish <note>original: "ruddy"</note> toad drink the juice of grapes so fast,
Until, overcharged with the liquid, his bowels all burst <note>original: "to brast"</note>:
And after that, from his poisoned body <note>original: "bulk"</note> he cast out his cruel <note>original: "fell"</note> venom,
Because of the grief and pain of which all his limbs began to swell;
With drops of poisoned sweat, approaching his secret den in this way,
He then turned his cave entirely white with blasts of smoky air:
And from that, after a time, a golden liquid <term>humour</term> followed,
Whose falling drops from above stained the soil with a reddish hue.
<note>In alchemy, the "Toad" often represents the *prima materia* (the base starting material) or the "Nigredo" (blackness) stage, while the "juice of grapes" represents the solvent or "mercurial water." The bursting and sweating describe the chemical reactions occurring within the sealed vessel.</note>

<summary>This page introduces an alchemical poem by Sir George Ripley, using the allegory of a toad that drinks too much wine, bursts, and emits a golden liquid to symbolize the early stages of the alchemical process.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, alchemy, The Vision, symbolic toad, Bridlington, transmutation, golden humour, prima materia</keywords>

--- Page 557 ---
<meta>This page begins the modernization of Sir George Ripley’s famous alchemical poem, "The Vision." The catchword from the previous page was "And."</meta>

# THE VISION OF SIR GEORGE RIPLEY,
### Canon of Bridlington, Unfolded. <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was a famous English alchemist and Augustinian canon at Bridlington Priory. "Unfolded" here means explained or revealed.</note>

When I was busy with my book one certain night,
This vision, expressed here, appeared to my dim sight:
I saw a toad, deep red <note>original: "full Ruddy"</note>, drinking the juice of grapes so fast,
Until, overcharged with the liquid <note>original: "broth"</note>, his bowels all burst <note>original: "to brast"</note>.
And after that, from his poisoned body <note>original: "Bulk"</note>, he vomited his deadly <note>original: "fell"</note> venom;
From the grief and pain of it, all his limbs <note>original: "Members"</note> began to swell.
With drops of poisoned sweat, as he approached his secret den,
He turned his cave completely white <note>original: "bewhited"</note> with blasts of smoky <note>original: "fumous"</note> air.
And from that cave, in time, a golden liquid <note>original: "Humour"</note> emerged,
Whose falling drops from above stained the soil with a deep red <note>original: "ruddy"</note> hue.

<summary>This page introduces a famous alchemical poem by George Ripley, using the allegory of a red toad to describe the chemical and spiritual stages of the "Great Work," including dissolution and the emergence of a golden substance.</summary>
<keywords>George Ripley, Alchemy, Alchemical Allegory, The Red Toad, Bridlington, Hermeticism, Transmutation, Golden Humour</keywords>

--- Page 558 ---
<meta>This page continues the modernization of Sir George Ripley’s poem, "The Vision," following the description of the toad bursting and staining the earth. The text describes the final stages of the alchemical process: decomposition, purification, and the creation of the philosopher's stone.</meta>

### The Vision of Sir George Ripley.

*And when his body began to lack the force*
*of vital breath,*
**This dying toad immediately became as black**
**as coal in color:**
*Thus drowned in his own veins of a*
*poisoned flood;*
**For a period of eighty-four days he**
**stood rotting.**
*Then, by trial <note>original: "Tryal"; here meaning a chemical experiment or testing process</note>, I desired to expel*
*this venom;*
**For which I committed his carcass to a**
**gentle fire:**
*Which being done—a wonder to see, but more*
*so to be retold <note>original: "rehears't"</note>;*
**The toad was pierced through every side**
**with rare colors;**
*And white appeared when all the various <note>original: "sundry"</note>*
*hues were past:*
**Which after being tinted deep red <note>original: "Ruddy"</note>, lasted**
**forever.**
*Then from the venom handled this way, a medicine*
*I did make;*
**Which venom kills, and saves those who**
**happen to take venom:** <note>This refers to the alchemical paradox where the "poison" of the raw material is transformed into a universal cure or "panacea."</note>
**Glory be to Him, the granter of such**
**secret ways,**
**Dominion and honor both, with**
**worship and with praise. Amen.**

<summary>This concluding section of Ripley’s vision describes the alchemical stages of Nigredo (blackness/decay), Albedo (whiteness/purification), and Rubedo (redness/perfection), resulting in a "medicine" that both kills and heals.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, alchemy, The Great Work, Nigredo, Albedo, Rubedo, transmutation, philosopher's stone, symbolic medicine</keywords>

### Canon of *Bridlington*, Unfolded.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'T' featuring floral patterns and foliage.</image-desc>

This vision is more like a parable or an enigma <note>original: "Enigm"; a puzzling or mysterious statement or picture</note>, which the ancient wise philosophers were often accustomed to use when setting out their secrets. All people are granted the freedom to use enigmatic expressions to explain that which is indeed mysterious. The ancient Egyptians taught much through hieroglyphics, a method that many fathers of this science <note>referring to the "science" of alchemy</note> have followed; but they have most especially made use of mystical or kabbalistic <note>original: "Cabaliſtical"; referring to the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Renaissance alchemists to hide secret meanings</note> descriptions. This text is one of those. But now, let us get to the matter at hand.

<summary>This page introduces a commentary on Sir George Ripley’s alchemical poem, explaining that such "visions" are deliberate enigmas used by philosophers to protect mysterious truths, similar to Egyptian hieroglyphics.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Bridlington, alchemy, parable, enigma, hieroglyphics, kabbalistic, hermetic philosophy, symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the prose exposition of Sir George Ripley's "The Vision." The previous page established that the poem is an alchemical enigma, using symbolic language to hide scientific secrets. This page begins to decode the specific symbol of the "Toad."</meta>

<header>An Exposition upon</header>

### *A Toad full ruddy I saw.*—

Here we have a Toad described, and within it lies the entire secret of the Philosophers: The Toad is Gold. It is called this because it is an earthly body, but most especially because of the black, foul-smelling toxicity <term>original: "venenosity"; a poisonous or toxic quality</term> which develops during the first days of its preparation, before the whiteness appears. During the "Rule of Saturn" <note>In alchemy, the "Rule of Saturn" refers to the first stage of the "Great Work," characterized by the color black, known as the *nigredo*, or putrefaction</note>, it is therefore called the ruddy <note>original: "ruddy"; reddish-brown or blood-colored</note> Toad.

On this point, authors agree unanimously when they say our "stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold</note> is nothing else but Gold refined <term>original: "digested"; a process of slow, steady heat used to mature or chemically change a substance</term> to the highest degree that Nature and Art can achieve. Furthermore, another philosopher says the first task is to purify <term>original: "sublime"; the process of heating a solid until it vaporizes and then cooling it back into a solid to remove impurities</term> *Mercury*, and then to place pure "bodies" <note>referring to solid metallic elements, likely gold or silver</note> into that clean *Mercury*. I could bring many witnesses to support this; indeed, the entire consensus of writers follows this path. And what if some clever *Philosophers* seem to deny this, intentionally trying to mislead the unsuspecting reader? We will not make it our task to reconcile their differences (though we could), for many of them wrote with a spirit of jealousy <term>original: "enviously"; in alchemical texts, this refers to a desire to hide secrets from the "unworthy" or uninitiated</term> on purpose to ensnare the reader; all of them wrote mysteri— <meta>catchword: mysteriously</meta>

<summary>The author explains that the "Toad" in Ripley’s vision represents gold undergoing the first stage of alchemical transformation, where it becomes black and "toxic" before being purified. He asserts that the Philosopher’s Stone is made from gold and mercury, despite some authors writing obscurely to protect their secrets.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Toad, Gold, Saturn, nigredo, Philosopher's Stone, Mercury, George Ripley, symbolism, purification</keywords>

--- Page 561 ---
<meta>This page continues the prose commentary on Sir George Ripley's "The Vision," an alchemical poem. Having previously identified the "Toad" as a symbol for Gold in its initial state of "putrefaction" (the blackening stage), the author now explains why the alchemists used such obscure language and begins to define the "juice of grapes" mentioned in the poem.</meta>

# the Vision of Sir G. Ripley.

...mysteriously, as much as they could to obscure <note>original: "darken"</note> the truth. At best, none of them were anything more than men, and they described things according to their own understanding of philosophy. None of them wrote the naked truth in every detail; for if they had, the Art <note>Alchemy</note> would have become so easy that it would be despised <term>original: "contemned"</term>. But why use more words? We know the Truth, and we can distinguish true writers from deceivers <term>original: "Sophisters"</term> by a secret sign <term>original: "Character"</term>. We need no further arguments, being eye-witnesses ourselves, and we know there is but one truth and but one path—the well-trodden path in which everyone who has ever mastered this Art has walked. We cannot be deceived ourselves, nor would we deceive others.

->*Did drink the juice of Grapes.*<-

This Toad is said to "drink the juice of Grapes." According to the philosopher, the body is no nobler than Gold, nor is the water more precious than wine. This water they sometimes call *Burning Water* <note>original: "Aqua Ardens"; a term often used for distilled alcohol or a powerful chemical solvent</note>, sometimes *Sharpest Vinegar* <note>original: "Acetum Acerrimum"; referring to a highly concentrated acid</note>, but most commonly they call it their *Mercury*.

<summary>The author explains that alchemists used cryptic language to prevent their art from being trivialized and identifies the "juice of grapes" as the alchemical Mercury, also known as "Burning Water" or "Sharpest Vinegar."</summary>

<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Alchemy, Sophisters, juice of grapes, Toad, Gold, Aqua Ardens, Acetum Acerrimum, Mercury, philosophical solvents</keywords>

## An Exposition upon

Mastery, and that our Lion <term>In alchemy, the "Lion" often represents a strong metallic or mineral substance, such as "philosophical gold" or antimony, undergoing transformation.</term> has also a little infected them with his carcass, which they begin to prey upon. This operation is called by skilled alchemists "Extraction of Natures" and "Separation," for the tincture <term>The "tincture" is the concentrated essence or soul of the metal that can transform other substances.</term> begins now to be separated from the body. It is also called "Reduction to the first matter," which is sperm or seed; because of its double nature, it is compared to two dragons. I shall not expand further on this vision, but briefly unfold what is briefly laid down.

->*Whose falling drops from high did*<-
->*stain the soil with a reddish hue.*<-

These colors of the mercury affect the solid body below <note>original: "subsident fixed Body"</note> with matching colors. The bodies, through these vapors <note>original: "exhalations"</note>, are colored with a reddish tint. Nicolas Flamel <note>A famous 14th-century French scribe and legendary alchemist.</note> describes this by saying that these two natures, or dragons, bite one another very cruelly. They never let go from the moment they have seized one another until, through their dripping venom and mortal wounds, they are completely covered in gore. Then, being stewed in

--- Page 576 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical process of circulation and distillation, specifically describing the transition into the stage of putrefaction.</meta>

->An Exposition upon<-

...approaching. For this repeated rotation of the influences of the heavens <note>original: "Influences of the Heaven"; referring to the cyclical heating and cooling that mimics celestial patterns</note>, together with heat constantly drying up and soaking in the moisture as fast as it falls, eventually brings the substance naturally to die and decay, just as any other thing does.

And then the body <note>original: "Corps"</note> begins to lack breath; that is, the fumes begin to cease. Because of the frequent ascending and descending, the spirits <term>volatile substances or vapors</term> are somewhat fixed <term>solidified or made non-volatile through chemical processing</term> and turned into powder or dust. They are now at the bottom of the vessel, quickly approaching putrefaction <term>the stage of decomposition or blackening, considered essential for purification in alchemy</term>. For a time, they do not ascend, but remain below.

Therefore, control your fire so that your spirits are not so exalted <note>original: "exalted"; here meaning evaporated or driven off by excessive heat</note> and climb so high that the earth <note>the solid matter remaining at the bottom of the vessel</note> lacks them and they return no more. For this operation is, as *Morienus* <note>original: "Morien"; referring to Morienus Romanus, a legendary 12th-century alchemist reputed to have taught the secrets of the Philosopher's Stone</note> says, a drawing out of water from the earth and a returning of that same water to the earth, repeated so often and so long until the earth putrefies.

<summary>This page describes the alchemical stage where repeated distillation leads to "putrefaction," as the volatile vapors (spirits) become fixed into a solid powder at the bottom of the vessel. The author cautions the reader to regulate the heat carefully to ensure the vapors return to the base matter rather than escaping.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, putrefaction, spirits, Morienus, fixation, distillation, chemical vessel, heat regulation</keywords>

# The Vision of Sir George Ripley

-> *This dying toad immediately became as black as coal.* <note>original: "This dying Toad became forthwith like Coal for colour black"; in alchemy, the "toad" often symbolizes the raw, earthy, or toxic starting material undergoing transformation.</note> <-

This is the final end of the combat <note>referring to the chemical reaction or "struggle" between the opposing volatile and fixed elements</note>. For within this "Earth of Leaves" <term>also known as foliated earth; the flaky, solid residue that remains after distillation</term>, all elements are reconciled and a final peace is made. Now, one "nature" embraces another, taking no other form than a powder impalpable <note>a powder so fine it cannot be felt or detected by touch</note>, and no other color but the blackest of blacks.

From this point forward, the natures are united; they boil and bubble together like melted pitch <note>a thick, dark, tar-like resin</note> and change their forms from one into another. Take care, therefore, that instead of this "Black of the Blackest" powder—which is also called the "Crow’s Bill" <term>a traditional alchemical term for the stage of *nigredo*, or blackness, signifying the death of the old form</term>—you do not produce a useless, dry, semi-red precipitate <term>a solid that settles out of a liquid solution</term> of an orange color. Such a color is a certain sign of the "combustion of flowers" <note>the accidental burning or overheating of the substance's "vegetative" or life-giving essence</note>, or the destruction of the power of the vegetative seed. I have stumbled on this very rock myself, and I therefore warn you of it.

<summary>This page describes the transition of the alchemical matter into the "Crow's Bill," a state of perfect blackness and fine powder. The author warns the reader to control the heat carefully to avoid burning the substance into a useless orange precipitate.</summary>
<keywords>Sir George Ripley, Crow's Bill, Nigredo, Putrefaction, Earth of Leaves, Alchemical Combat, Precipitate, Combustion of Flowers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the final stages of the "nigredo" or blackness in the alchemical process, following the description of the "Crow's Bill."</meta>

# An Exposition upon the Vision

-> *Thus drowned in his own veins of a poisonous flood.* <note>original: "Thus drowned in his proper Veins of poy-soned Flood"; this refers to the substance being dissolved by its own internal acidic or "mercurial" nature.</note> <-

It appears from everything that has been said, and by the certain testimony of all philosophers <note>original: "Philosophers"; here referring to alchemists or natural scientists</note> who have been eyewitnesses to this truth, that the work is neither so tedious nor so expensive <note>original: "chargeable"</note> that the mastery of the craft cannot be attained through the simple way of nature. 

For once the true body is mixed into a paste <note>original: "Impasted"</note> with its true leaven <term>a substance that triggers transformation and "rising," much like yeast in bread dough</term>, it burns itself into ash <note>original: "calcine itself"</note> and dissolves itself. This dissolution of the body into a black and multi-colored water is the sign that the "tincture" <term>the spiritual or coloring essence of the matter</term> has emerged. This is the solidifying <note>original: "congelation"</note> of the spirits into this lowest point of darkness <note>original: "Obscurity"</note>, which results in a black powder like lamp-black. This is the completion of the "eclipse" <note>original: "Eclipsation"; the stage of total blackness or *nigredo* where the light of the matter is hidden</note>. This process of grinding down <note>original: "Contrition"</note> begins soon after the appearance of various colors, such as yellowish, bluish, and others.

<summary>The author explains that the alchemical "Mastery" is attainable through natural means, describing how the substance dissolves itself into a black powder—marking the completion of the "eclipse" or nigredo stage.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, nigredo, tincture, calcination, leaven, lamp-black, Sir George Ripley, Eirenaeus Philalethes</keywords>
